                                                              Expectation-Maximization for Low-SNR
                                                                    Multi-Reference Alignment
                                                           Amnon Balanov∗1 , Wasim Huleihel1 , and Tamir Bendory1
                                           1
                                               School of Electrical and Computer Engineering, Tel Aviv University, Tel Aviv 69978, Israel




arXiv:2505.21435v2 [eess.SP] 11 Feb 2026
                                                                                      February 12, 2026


                                                                                            Abstract
                                                          We study the multi-reference alignment (MRA) problem of recovering a signal from
                                                      noisy observations acted on by unknown random circular shifts. While the information-
                                                      theoretic limits of MRA are well characterized in many settings, the algorithmic be-
                                                      havior at low signal-to-noise ratio (SNR), the regime of practical interest, remains
                                                      poorly understood. In this paper, we analyze the expectation–maximization (EM) al-
                                                      gorithm, a widely used method for MRA, and characterize its convergence dynamics
                                                      and initialization dependence in the low-SNR limit.
                                                          On the convergence side, we prove a two-phase phenomenon near the ground truth
                                                      as SNR → 0: an initial contraction with error decaying as exp(− SNR · t) followed by
                                                      a much slower phase scaling as exp(− SNR2 · t), where t is the iteration number. This
                                                      yields an iteration-complexity lower bound T ≳ SNR−2 to reach a small fixed target
                                                      accuracy, revealing a severe computational bottleneck at low SNR. We also identify
                                                      a finite-sample instability, which we term Ghost of Newton, in which EM initially
                                                      approaches the ground truth but later diverges, degrading reconstruction quality.
                                                          On the bias side, we analyze EM in the noise-only setting (SNR = 0), a regime
                                                      referred to as Einstein from Noise, to highlight its pronounced sensitivity to initial-
                                                      ization. We prove that the EM map preserves the Fourier phases of the initialization
                                                      across all iterations, while the corresponding Fourier magnitudes contract toward zero
                                                      at a slow rate of (1 + T )−1/2 . Consequently, although the amplitudes vanish in the
                                                      limit of T → ∞ iterations, the reconstructed structure continues to reflect the geometry
                                                      encoded by the template’s Fourier phases. We complement this analysis by studying
                                                      model bias in the low-SNR regime, as well as finite-sample effects. Together, these
                                                      results expose fundamental computational and initialization-driven limitations of EM
                                                      for MRA in the low-SNR regime.

                                              4
1     Introduction
Reconstructing signals from noisy observations subject to unknown group transformations
is a fundamental challenge in numerous scientific fields, including signal processing, com-
puter vision, medical imaging, and structural biology. A widely studied abstraction of this
challenge is the multi-reference alignment (MRA) problem [9, 12, 47, 8].

1.1    Problem description
In this work, we study the classical MRA problem, where the objective is to recover an
unknown signal x⋆ ∈ Rd from noisy measurements consisting of randomly circularly shifted
copies of x⋆ . For each shift ℓ ∈ {0, 1, . . . , d − 1}, we define the circular-shift operator
Tℓ : Rd → Rd by

                   [Tℓ z]j ≜ z(j−ℓ) mod d ,    for any z ∈ Rd and 0 ≤ j ≤ d − 1.

Under this model, the observations are given by,

                               (The MRA Model) yi = Tℓi x⋆ + ξi ,                            (1.1)
                             i.i.d.
for 1 ≤ i ≤ n, where {ξi }i ∼ N (0, σ 2 Id ) are independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.)
Gaussian random variables with zero mean and variance σ 2 . The shifts {ℓi }i are drawn
independently and uniformly at random, each over the set {0, 1, . . . , d − 1}, independently of
{ξi }i . The objective is to recover the underlying signal x⋆ , up to a global circular shift, from
the noisy and randomly shifted observations {yi }ni=1 . Throughout, we define the signal-to-
noise ratio (SNR) by

                                                    ∥x⋆ ∥2
                                              SNR ≜        .                                 (1.2)
                                                     dσ 2
    At high SNR, one can often recover the underlying structure by first aligning the obser-
vations and then averaging them, for example via synchronization techniques [54, 53, 42].
However, in the low-SNR regime, which is common in many real-world applications and the
main focus of this work, reliable alignment becomes infeasible, as the noise overwhelms the
signal [18]. Despite this challenge in the low-SNR regime, it is still possible to recover the
underlying structure by modeling the unknown shifts as nuisance variables and applying
statistical inference techniques to marginalize them out.
The expectation-maximization algorithm. To estimate the unknown signal x⋆ in the
MRA model (1.1), a widely used approach is the expectation-maximization (EM) algo-
rithm [20, 51, 49]. In practice, EM underpins many MRA pipelines, most notably in cryo-
EM [3, 39, 45, 67] and cryo-electron tomography (cryo-ET) [17, 59, 64].
    EM is an iterative refinement scheme that alternates between (i) estimating, for each
observation, a probability distribution over all possible shifts (E-step), and (ii) updating the
current signal estimate as the corresponding probability-weighted average (M-step). Each
iteration is guaranteed to monotonically (but not necessarily strictly) increase the likelihood,
and the procedure converges to a stationary point of the likelihood.

                                                    5
    A well-known limitation of EM is its sensitivity to initialization [16, 6]. In practice, the
iterations start from an initial template xtemplate , chosen either at random or using domain
knowledge. While a good template can accelerate convergence and improve reconstruc-
tion quality [55, 11], finding one is often challenging. Moreover, handcrafted templates can
introduce confirmation bias, potentially steering reconstructions toward preconceived struc-
tures [24, 7, 6]. This issue is especially pronounced in cryo-electron microscopy (cryo-EM),
where substantial effort has been devoted to designing effective initialization strategies for
EM, e.g., [10, 31].
The sample complexity of the MRA problem. Recent theoretical advances have
shown that, even when individual translations cannot be reliably estimated, the underlying
signal in the MRA problem can still be recovered provided that the number of observations is
sufficiently large [1, 41, 8]. In the asymptotic regime where both the number of observations
n → ∞ and the SNR → 0, a necessary condition for unique recovery is that the number of
samples satisfies n = ω(SNR−3 ), that is, n/SNR−3 → ∞, as n → ∞, and SNR → 0; see
Section A.2 for further discussion. Moreover, this information-theoretic scaling is achieved
by method-of-moments based estimators, which recover x⋆ via low-order moments such as
autocorrelations and the bispectrum [41, 12]. However, these sample-complexity guarantees
address identifiability rather than algorithmic performance: they do not imply that the
EM algorithm will converge to the maximum-likelihood estimate (MLE) or yield accurate
estimates. Despite this gap, EM remains a widely used workhorse for MRA and related
latent-group models, making it important to understand its behavior in the high-noise, low-
SNR regime, where its practical performance is still far from fully characterized.
The EM algorithm in the low SNR regime. Recent studies have begun to shed light
on this gap. Fan et al. [23] showed that the likelihood landscape of discrete orbit-recovery
models, i.e., latent-group models in which observations are transformed by an unknown
element of a finite group (such as cyclic shifts in MRA), can contain spurious local optima
that may trap EM iterations. Extending this analysis to the cryo-EM setting, they further
demonstrated that the Fisher information captures intrinsic group-invariant degeneracies
that slow or prevent convergence [22]. Complementary work by Katsevich and Bandeira [26]
revealed that in the low-SNR regime, EM behaves approximately as a moment-matching
algorithm, offering additional insight into its local dynamics. Despite these advances, the
quantitative convergence rate of EM at low SNR remains insufficiently characterized.

1.2    Main results
In this work, we develop a unified theory of EM for MRA in low-SNR regimes, showing
that the EM dynamics exhibit complementary behaviors as a function of both the SNR and
the initialization scale. Table 1 provides a schematic overview of the regimes studied, and
summarizes the corresponding contributions in both the population limit (n → ∞) and the
finite-sample setting (finite n < ∞).
    We next elaborate on the key results in each regime below. Throughout, we write x̂(t)
for the t-th EM iterate, x̂(0) for the initialization, and X̂(t) [k] for the k-th discrete Fourier
coefficient of x̂(t) . We complement the theoretical analysis with population-level numerical
experiments. In these simulations, we evaluate the expectations defining the population

(typically d = 5) to keep the quadrature computations tractable.

1.2.1   Convergence in the vicinity of ground-truth
The first part of this work (Section 3) studies the behavior of EM in a neighborhood of the
ground-truth in the low-SNR regime, focusing on the population EM map.
Two phases of convergence. Figure 1(a) highlights a striking phenomenon: EM exhibits
two phases of convergence in the vicinity of the ground-truth: first, a moderate convergence
rate regime with decay proportional to exp (−c SNR · t) followed by a markedly slower phase
                  ′    2
                           
scaling as exp −c SNR · t . We provide a theoretical explanation of this behavior in Sec-
tion 3 and Theorem 3.6.
Iteration complexity. As a direct consequence of this two-phase convergence behavior,
Section 3.3 derives iteration-complexity bounds for EM in MRA. In particular, even within
the vicinity of the ground-truth, EM requires at least T ≳ SNR−2 iterations to attain
any fixed accuracy, so the runtime degrades significantly as the noise level increases. We
summarize these findings informally below; see Corollary 3.8 for iteration complexity.

Theorem 1.1 (Informal: Iteration complexity of EM in MRA). In the low-SNR regime of
the MRA model (1.1) (i.e., SNR → 0), EM needs at least T ≳ SNR−2 iterations to reach a
small, fixed target accuracy.

Figure 1: Population EM dynamics in the low-SNR regime. (a) Two-phase convergence
at positive 0 < SNR ≪ 1. The plot shows the normalized population MSE ∥x̂(t) − x⋆ ∥22 /∥x∥22 versus
iteration t for several low SNR values (annotated on the curves). The population EM map exhibits
a clear two-phase behavior: an initial moderate-rate geometric decay, MSE ∝ exp (−c SNR · t),
followed by a slow phase where the error decreases much more slowly, MSE ∝ exp −c′ SNR2 · t .
                                                                                                        

(b) Fourier magnitudes contraction to 0 at SNR = 0. The Fourier magnitudes |X̂(t) [k]|2 are shown
for a representative non-mean Fourier mode under the population EM operator. The dashed curve
is the closed-form prediction from Theorem 5.3, |X̂(t) [k]|2 /|X̂(0) [k]|2 = (1 + 2t|X̂(0) [k]|2 )−1 . The
agreement corroborate that, in the regime of SNR = 0, Fourier magnitudes decay toward zero at
a rate ≍ (1 + t)−1 , while the corresponding Fourier phases remain frozen along the population
trajectory (not shown in the Figure). Simulation parameters: Population expectations defining the
EM update are evaluated numerically via Gauss-Hermite quadrature over the Gaussian noise with
a fixed signal dimension d = 5.


1.2.2                                      Bias to initialization at low-SNR
In addition to the local convergence analysis, we characterize a complementary bias to ini-
tialization regime of EM in the low (but non-vanishing) SNR setting (Section 4). Whereas
the convergence results describe the dynamics once the iterate is already close to the ground
truth, the bias to initialization regime applies more broadly whenever the EM initialization
has small norm relative to the noise level. Theorem 4.1 shows that while the mean component
is estimated after a single iteration, the remaining population drift in the other non-mean
components is extremely weak: the displacement per iteration is O(SNR). Consequently,
the EM “forgets” its initialization only on a long time scale: achieving a constant fraction
change relative to ∥x̂(0) ∥ requires at least T ≳ SNR−1 iterations.

1.2.3                                      Model bias at vanishing SNR
A complementary contribution of this work is a quantitative analysis of the Einstein from
Noise phenomenon as a form of algorithmic confirmation bias. In the extreme case where
SNR = 0 (i.e., when the signal is absent), the reconstruction tends to align with the initial
template (the user’s prior guess) over many iterations. This phenomenon may also be viewed

                                                                                         8
as the limiting case of vanishing signal strength: SNR → 0 while the initialization x̂(0)
remains nonvanishing (i.e., does not scale to zero with the signal). While this effect was
previously observed and studied in single-iteration hard-assignment algorithms [50, 51, 7],
in this work, we extend the analysis to the EM algorithm across multiple iterations.
    Our analysis characterizes the Einstein from Noise dynamics both for finite-dimensional
signals d ∈ N and the asymptotic signal length d → ∞. For fixed dimension d, Theorem 5.1
shows that each population EM step acts diagonally in the Fourier basis: all non-mean
Fourier phases are preserved from one iteration to the next, while their magnitudes contract.
Consequently, Corollary 5.2 establishes that along the entire population trajectory the phases
remain identical to those of the initialization, even as the iterates converge to the origin.
Moreover, Theorem 5.3 yields an explicit contraction law: for any fixed non-mean frequency
k and iteration T ,

                                |X̂(T ) [k]|        1
                                            =q                 .                            (1.3)
                                |X̂(0) [k]|             (0)
                                              1 + 2T |X̂ [k]|2


This result empirically matches the convergence of the population EM as seen in Figure 1(b).
Thus, at SNR = 0, the Fourier magnitudes decay toward zero as T → ∞, but only at the
slow rate ≍ (1 + T )−1/2 , while the Fourier phases remain identical to their initialized values.

1.2.4   Finite-sample analysis
We also study the finite-sample manifestations of the regimes described above. For finite-
sample numerical studies (Monte-Carlo experiments), we use a running example that appears
throughout the paper (Figure 2). The ground-truth signal x⋆ is a grayscale image of Newton;
each observation is generated by applying a random 2D cyclic shift to x⋆ and then adding
Gaussian noise. We compare two iterative procedures: (i) the standard EM algorithm, and
(ii) a hard-assignment variant [11] which, at each iteration, assigns each yi to its most likely
transformation Tℓi given the current estimate, and then updates the signal by averaging the
aligned observations. Both methods require initialization, and in all experiments we use a
grayscale image of Einstein as a representative initial template.
The Ghost of Newton phenomenon. We identify a finite-sample artifact, which we
term the Ghost of Newton (named after the Newton image used as ground truth in our
running example). This phenomenon appears at intermediate SNR levels and can be seen
when the EM algorithm initially converges toward the correct structure (Newton), but sub-
sequent iterations introduce a degradation in accuracy (see Figure 4). This results in a
non-monotonic MSE curve: the error initially decreases as the reconstruction improves, then
increases as the estimate begins to diverge from the ground truth (Figure 4(a)).
    The underlying mechanism is the discrepancy between the empirical and population EM
operators. In the population setting (n → ∞), and with an initialization in the vicinity of
the ground-truth, EM converges to the true signal x⋆ . For finite n, however, the empirical
EM map converges instead to a sample-dependent fixed point x̂⋆n that maximizes the finite-
sample likelihood. Consequently, when initialized near x⋆ , the empirical trajectory initially
tracks the population dynamics but eventually peels away under accumulated finite-sample
perturbations, drifting toward x̂⋆n . An analysis of this effect is provided in Section 6.2.

Figure 2: Empirical setup used in this work and reconstruction regimes. (a) Moti-
vating application: Illustration of the MRA model (1.1) in a two-dimensional setting. The goal
is to reconstruct a 2D Newton image from noisy, randomly shifted observations, starting from an
initial template of Einstein’s image. We study the expectation-maximization (EM) algorithm as
the primary method analyzed in this work, and include a hard-assignment method as a baseline for
comparison. (b) Reconstruction performance for both methods is quantified by the MSE, defined
in (2.4), and plotted as a function of SNR under cyclic 2D shifts. Four distinct regimes are observed:
1) Einstein from Noise: at very low SNR, the reconstruction resembles the initial template (Ein-
stein); 2) Ghost of Newton: at moderately low SNR, the EM algorithm initially converges to the
true structure (Newton), but in later iterations, the noise term causes the reconstruction to gradu-
ally deteriorate, leading to increasingly corrupted estimates (see Figure 4 for further illustration).
3) a transition region, 4) high-SNR regime, where alignment is accurate and performance approaches
the baseline case, following the scaling law MSE ∝ 1/SNR. All experiments use d = 64 × 64 images,
n = 2 × 104 observations, T = 200 iterations, and average results over 30 Monte Carlo trials per
data point.



                                                                                     10
Finite-sample Einstein from Noise. Finally, we turn to the finite-sample Einstein from
Noise regime. Proposition 6.5 shows that each empirical EM step introduces a small but
nonzero phase drift: the phase MSE between successive iterates is of order 1/n. Proposi-
tion 6.7 further shows that these per-iteration drifts accumulate at most quadratically in the
iteration count, yielding a template-referenced phase error that scales as ≍ T 2 /n over the
first T iterations.


2     Preliminaries and fundamental properties
This section introduces the notation, problem setup, and EM framework used throughout
the paper. We first present the EM algorithm and define the associated fixed-point maps:
the population operator M and its empirical counterpart Mn . For context, we also describe
a widely used alternative, the hard-assignment method, which can be viewed as a simplified
(“poor-man’s”) version of EM. We then prove a one-step consistency result in the high-SNR
regime, which leads to global convergence from arbitrary initializations.

2.1    Notation
              D   P    a.s.          Lp
Throughout, −      →, −→, −−→, and −→ denote convergence in distribution, in probability, almost
                     p
surely, and in L , respectively, for sequences of random variables. We denote the index set
{0, 1, . . . , d − 1} by [d], and Zd for the cyclic group of order d. For vectors and matrices
over R or C, (·)⊤ denotes the (ordinary) transpose. The notation (·)∗ denotes the Hermitian
transpose (conjugate transpose). The Euclidean inner product is written as either ⟨a, b⟩ or
a⊤ b when a, b ∈√Rd , and as ⟨a, b⟩ ≜ a∗ b when a, b ∈ Cd . We use ∥ · ∥2 for thep
                                                                                 vector Euclidean
norm, ∥x∥2 = x x, and ∥ · ∥F for the matrix Frobenius norm, ∥A∥F = trace(A∗ A); for
                       ∗

vectors, ∥x∥F = ∥x∥2 . When no confusion can arise, we write ∥ · ∥ for ∥ · ∥2 .
Asymptotic notations. We employ the following standard asymptotic notation. For
nonnegative sequences an and bn , we write an = O(bn ) if there exists a constant C < ∞ and
n0 such that an ≤ Cbn for all n ≥ n0 , and an = o(bn ) if an /bn → 0. We write an = ω(bn ) if
an /bn → ∞, and an = Θ(bn ) if both an = O(bn ) and bn = O(an ) hold.
    We also use the notation an ≳ bn to denote bn = O(an ); equivalently, there exist constants
c > 0 and n0 such that an ≥ cbn for all n ≥ n0 . Finally, we write an ≍ bn as a synonym for
an = Θ(bn ), that is, when an ≳ bn and bn ≳ an both hold. All asymptotic statements are
taken in the appropriate limit specified in context (e.g., n → ∞, SNR → 0, or d → ∞).
Fourier-space notation. To facilitate the analysis of EM for the MRA model, we work
in the discrete Fourier domain. For a complex number Z ∈ C, let ϕZ ≜ arg(Z) denote its
phase. Let F ∈ Cd×d be the unitary discrete Fourier transform (DFT) matrix with columns

2.2    Reconstruction algorithms
We present the EM algorithm as the primary iterative method studied in this work. For
comparison, we also introduce the popular hard-assignment approach. The two methods
differ in the objective functions they effectively optimize and, more fundamentally, in how
they treat uncertainty in the latent variables (i.e., the unknown cyclic shifts): EM uses soft
responsibilities, whereas hard assignment commits to a single shift estimate at each iteration.
EM algorithm. The EM algorithm, described in Algorithm 1, adopts a probabilistic ap-
proach to shift estimation [20]. At each iteration t, with current estimate x̂(t) , the algorithm
proceeds in two steps. First, it computes the posterior distribution over all shifts Tℓ for
each observation yi , conditioned on x̂(t) (E-step). Second, it updates the signal estimate
by computing a weighted average of the observations, where the weights reflect the shift
probabilities (M-step), resulting in the next iterate x̂(t+1) .
   The EM algorithm maximizes the log-likelihood of the data under the MRA model:
                        n       d−1                             !
                                                   ∥yi − Tℓ x∥2
                                                
                      X         X                                     dn
            L(x, Y) ≜      log      p(Tℓ ) · exp −         2
                                                                   −     log(2πσ 2 ),        (2.7)
                       i=1      ℓ=0
                                                        2σ             2

where p(Tℓ ) = 1/d reflects a uniform prior over shifts. The maximum likelihood estimator is
defined as

                                    x̂ML (Y) ≜ argmax L(x, Y).                               (2.8)
                                                  x∈Rd

Although alternative optimization strategies may be used to minimize L, the EM algorithm
is a practical and widely adopted method. It enjoys the desirable property of non-decreasing
likelihood across iterations [20]. By accounting for the full posterior distribution over shifts,
the EM algorithm offers greater robustness to noise than the hard-assignment method. This
increased robustness, however, comes at the cost of higher computational complexity, since
the algorithm requires accounting for all possible shifts for each observation. Additional
details and derivation of the EM update rule are provided in Appendix A.1.
Hard-assignment algorithm. The hard-assignment method, described in Algorithm 2,
provides a simple iterative procedure for estimating the signal x⋆ . At each iteration t, it
assigns to each observation yi the cyclic shift Tℓi that best aligns it with the current estimate
x̂(t) , that is, the shift minimizing the Euclidean distance ∥yi − Tℓ x̂(t) ∥. After aligning all
observations according to their assigned shifts, the estimate is updated by averaging the
aligned data to produce x̂(t+1) .
      This algorithm can be interpreted as an alternating minimization scheme for the following
non-convex objective function:
                                                 n
                                                 X
                              Lhard (x, Y) ≜           min ∥yi − Tℓi x∥2 .                    (2.9)
                                                       Tℓi ∈Zd
                                                 i=1

The objective is to optimize the function with respect to the signal:

                                 x̂hard (Y) ≜ argmin Lhard (x, Y).                           (2.10)
                                                   x∈Rd

In particular, the algorithm alternates between (i) estimating, for each observation, the shift
that best aligns it with the current signal estimate, and (ii) updating the signal estimate
given these inferred shifts. As with the EM case, other optimization strategies may be

used to maximize Lhard , but the hard-assignment algorithm is widely used in practice due
to its simplicity. The hard-assignment algorithm is computationally efficient and performs
well in moderate-noise regimes, where the selection of the most likely shift per observation is
typically correct. However, its performance degrades in high-noise settings due to its reliance
on a single shift per observation.

2.3    Population and finite-sample EM dynamics
In modern analyses of the EM algorithm, it is standard to distinguish between two related
operators: the population EM map, in which all expectations are taken under the true
data-generating distribution (equivalently, the limit corresponding to infinitely many sam-
ples, n → ∞), and the finite-sample or empirical EM map, in which these expectations
are replaced by empirical averages over the observed dataset [4, 19]. This distinction en-
ables a precise separation between the deterministic population dynamics and the random
fluctuations arising from finite-sample estimation.
Finite-sample EM operator. Recall that Y = {yi }ni=1 denote n i.i.d. observations drawn
from the MRA model (1.1). The empirical EM operator Mn : Rd → Rd , corresponds to the
empirical update rule introduced in (2.6), is defined by
                                                 n    d−1
                                             1 XX
                                                       γℓ (x; yi ) Tℓ−1 yi ,
                                                                          
                             Mn (x; Y) =                                                     (2.13)
                                             n i=1 ℓ=0

where γℓ (x; yi ), defined in (2.5), denotes the posterior responsibility of shift ℓ given the
current signal estimate x, and Tℓ is the circular-shift operator. The finite-sample EM iteration
then proceeds as

                             x̂(t+1) = Mn x̂(t) ; Y ,

so the empirical operator coincides almost surely with the population operator in the infinite-

M is deterministic.

we analyze the finite-sample behavior, we explicitly distinguish between the empirical and
population EM updates using the notation in (2.14) and (2.16), respectively.

2.4     Global convergence at high-SNR
Here, we analyze the asymptotic high-SNR regime, i.e., in the low-noise limit σ → 0 (equiv-
alently, SNR → ∞) with d fixed. In this limit, the posterior over shifts concentrates on the
maximizer of the cross-correlation1 , so the EM update reduces to a hard alignment step. As
a consequence, EM and hard assignment become asymptotically identical, and both recover
the ground-truth orbit after a single iteration. This asymptotic prediction is consistent with
the empirical behavior at moderately high SNR (small but nonzero σ): in that regime both
algorithms converge rapidly after a few iterations and exhibit essentially indistinguishable
reconstructions.

Proposition 2.1 (Global convergence at high-SNR). Let {yi }ni=1 be observations drawn
                                                                     (t+1)      (t+1)
from the MRA model in (1.1), with ground-truth signal x⋆ . Let x̂hard and x̂EM denote the
reconstructions after t + 1 iterations of the hard-assignment (Algorithm 2) and EM (Algo-
rithm 1), respectively. Denote x̂(0) = xtemplate as the initialization used by both algorithms.
Assume that the cross-correlation between x⋆ and x̂(t) attains a unique maximum, that is
argmaxℓ∈[d] ⟨x⋆ , Tℓ x̂(t) ⟩ is unique. Then,
   1
    In our setting, the hard-assignment rule in (2.9) can be written equivalently as either minimizing an ℓ2
distance or maximizing a cross-correlation since each shift operator Tℓ is orthonormal, and it preserves the
Euclidean norm, ∥Tℓ x∥2 = ∥x∥2 .



                                                     15
    1. For any n ∈ N, the EM and hard-assignment reconstructions are asymptotically equiv-
       alent in the low-noise limit, i.e.,
                                                (t)         (t)
                                         lim x̂EM − x̂hard = 0,                            (2.18)
                                        σ→0

      almost surely, for every t ≥ 0.

    2. For any n ∈ N,
                                                      (1)
                                        lim dorb (x̂hard , x⋆ ) = 0,                       (2.19)
                                        σ→0

      almost surely.

    The proof of Proposition 2.1 is given in Appendix H for a broad family of models that
includes (1.1). It builds on [46, Theorem 5.10] and extends the argument from cyclic shifts
to more general group actions. While in the high-SNR limit, both EM and hard assignment
recover the ground-truth orbit from arbitrary initializations, the remainder of the paper is
devoted to the low-SNR regime, where global convergence fails and the dynamics are instead
governed by initialization bias and finite-sample drift.


3     EM convergence at low SNR
In this section we characterize the behavior of the population EM estimator in the low-SNR
regime. We begin by establishing a spectral-radius criterion for local linear convergence
of the population EM map M via the Jacobian J(x⋆ ) evaluated at the ground truth x⋆ .
Building on this, we derive a low-SNR Taylor expansion of J(x⋆ ) and obtain a spectral
decomposition that governs the dynamics of EM in this regime. These tools yield two
main results: Theorem 3.6, which explains the two-phase convergence behavior observed in
Figure 1, and Corollary 3.8, which quantifies the iteration complexity of EM at low SNR.
For completeness, Appendix I also provides a global (i.e., not necessarily in the vicinity of
the ground-truth) low-SNR approximation of the population EM map.

3.1     Local convergence of the population EM
We first characterize the local convergence behavior of the population EM dynamics defined
in (2.15). It is well established that the local behavior of the EM algorithm around a fixed
point is governed by the spectral properties of the Jacobian of the population update opera-
tor. Classical analyzes (see, for instance, [66, 4, 19, 35, 44]), show that if the spectral radius
of the Jacobian at the true parameter is strictly smaller than one, the population EM itera-
tion converges linearly to the fixed point. Moreover, directions corresponding to eigenvalues
close to one give rise to slow convergence modes. Our goal in this section is to establish
an analogous characterization for the MRA model, demonstrating that its population EM
operator exhibits the same spectral radius-based local convergence behavior.




                                                16
Jacobian, spectral radius, and symmetry considerations. Before analyzing the con-
vergence of the population EM dynamics, we formalize the notions of the Jacobian and its
spectral radius, which govern the local behavior of iterative algorithms. Detailed prelimi-
naries and results on nonlinear dynamical systems can be found in Appendix B.

Definition 3.1 (Jacobian and spectral radius). Let F : Rd → Rd be a continuously differ-
entiable multivariate function in a neighborhood of a point x⋆ ∈ Rd . The Jacobian matrix
of F at x⋆ is the linear map

                                  JF (x⋆ ) ≜ ∇F (x) x=x⋆ ∈ Rd×d ,                          (3.1)

characterized by the first-order expansion: for any vector h ∈ Rd with ∥h∥2 → 0,

                          F (x⋆ + h) − F (x⋆ ) = JF (x⋆ ) h + o(∥h∥2 ).                    (3.2)

For a square matrix J ∈ Rd×d , we denote by spec(J) ⊂ C its spectrum, i.e., the set of
eigenvalues of J. The spectral radius of J is then defined as

                                 ρ(J) ≜ max{ |λ| : λ ∈ spec(J) }.                          (3.3)

Symmetry of the EM operator. In the MRA model (1.1) with uniform distribution
of cyclic shifts, the log-likelihood and hence the EM update operator are equivariant with
respect to circular shifts (see Lemma C.1), namely,

                        M (Tℓ x) = Tℓ M (x),          ∀ℓ ∈ {0, 1, . . . d − 1}.            (3.4)

and the ground-truth signal x⋆ is not an isolated fixed point but lies on an orbit of equivalent
solutions

                            Orb(x⋆ ) = {Tℓ x⋆ : ℓ = 0, 1, . . . , d − 1}.                  (3.5)

This inherent symmetry implies that the EM fixed points are not unique: every circularly
shifted version of x⋆ yields the same likelihood value and defines an equivalent solution.
To analyze local convergence meaningfully, one must therefore disambiguate among these
equivalent representatives. Thus, we restrict the dynamics to a canonical neighborhood of
a single representative of the orbit by introducing a condition that guarantees the identity
shift is the nearest orbit element in a small neighborhood.

Assumption 3.2. Let x⋆ ∈ Rd denote the ground–truth signal, and let {Tℓ }d−1  ℓ=0 be the finite
set of circular shifts acting on Rd . Assume there exists a radius r > 0 such that, for every
x in the open ball B(x⋆ , r) ≜ {x ∈ Rd : ∥x − x⋆ ∥2 < r}, the nearest shifted version of x⋆ is
uniquely attained at the identity shift:

                        argmin ∥x − Tℓ x⋆ ∥2 = {0},           ∀x ∈ B(x⋆ , r).              (3.6)
                         0≤ℓ<d

In other words, x⋆ is locally separated from all its nontrivial circular shifts.


                                                 17
    We are now ready to state the local linear convergence theorem for the MRA model;
its proof is given in Appendix C. The first part of the theorem is specific to the MRA
model (1.1), whereas the second and third parts follow from standard results in nonlinear
dynamical systems; see Appendix B for background.
Theorem 3.3 (Local linear convergence of the population EM operator). Assume that the
observation Y is generated according to the MRA model (1.1) with a uniform distribution over
cyclic shifts, and let the ground-truth signal x⋆ ∈ Rd . Suppose further that Assumption 3.2
holds. Let M : Rd → Rd denote the population EM operator defined in (2.15). Then:
  1. (Regularity and Jacobian of M ). The mapping M ∈ C ∞ (Rd ) is smooth on Rd . For
     any x ∈ Rd , its Jacobian J ∈ Rd×d is
                                           " d−1                               #
                                        1   X

                     J(x) ≜ ∇x M (x) = 2 E       γℓ (x; Y )(zℓ − z̄)(zℓ − z̄) ,  (3.7)
                                       σ     ℓ=0

                                         ℓ=0 γℓ (x; Y )zℓ . In particular, J(x ) is a real sym-
      metric matrix, hence orthogonally diagonalizable, and spec(J(x⋆ )) ⊂ [0, 1].

  2. (Local linear convergence). If ρ(J(x⋆ )) < 1, then for any constant q ∈ (ρ(J(x⋆ )), 1),
     there exists δ > 0 and a neighborhood U = B(x⋆ , δ) of x⋆ , such that for any initializa-
     tion x̂(0) ∈ U, the EM iterates x̂(t+1) = M (x̂(t) ) satisfy

                         ∥x̂(t) − x⋆ ∥2 ≤ q t ∥x̂(0) − x⋆ ∥2 ,           t = 0, 1, 2, . . . ,    (3.8)

  3. (Infinitesimal initialization regime). Let u1 be a unit eigenvector associated with the
     largest eigenvalue of J(x⋆ ), λ1 = ρ (J(x⋆ )). Consider EM iterates initialized at x̂(0)
     with a small but generic error, in the sense that x̂(0) → x⋆ and ⟨x̂(0) − x⋆ , u1 ⟩ ̸= 0.
     Then,

                                                     1    ∥x̂(t) − x⋆ ∥2
                        lim          lim               log (0)      ⋆
                                                                         = log ρ (J(x⋆ )) .     (3.10)
                        t→∞        x̂(0) →x⋆         t    ∥x̂ − x ∥2
                              ⟨x̂(0) −x⋆ , u1 ⟩̸=0


    Theorem 3.3 characterizes the local behavior of the population EM operator through the
eigenstructure of the Jacobian at the fixed point x⋆ . Item (1) establishes smoothness of M
and provides an explicit expression for the Jacobian, implying in particular that J(x⋆ ) is sym-
metric with spectrum contained in [0, 1]. Item (2) is a uniform local result: if ρ (J(x⋆ )) < 1,
then for any q ∈ (ρ(J(x⋆ )) , 1) there exists δ > 0 such that M is a contraction on the ball
U = B(x⋆ , δ); hence, for any initialization x̂(0) ∈ U, the EM iterates converge linearly to x⋆ ,
and the asymptotic contraction rate is at most ρ(J(x⋆ )). Item (3) is a sharp asymptotic
statement in the infinitesimal-initialization regime: for initializations approaching x⋆ with a

                                                         18
generic component along the top eigenvector u1 , the per-iteration decay rate converges to
log ρ(J(x⋆ )), i.e., the long-run behavior is dominated by the slowest contracting eigendirec-
tion. Overall, these guarantees are inherently local: they apply only to initializations within
a sufficiently small neighborhood around x⋆ (a local basin contained in the basin of attrac-
tion). The size and geometry of this neighborhood depend on higher-order terms beyond the
Jacobian (i.e., on nonlinear terms of M ) and are not captured by the linearization alone.

3.2    Spectral decomposition of the Jacobian
Having established in Theorem 3.3 that the local convergence rate of the population EM
operator is governed by the spectral radius of its Jacobian at the ground-truth x⋆ , we now
quantify this rate in the low-SNR regime of the MRA model (1.1). We parameterize the
ground truth as

                                    x⋆ = βv,           ∥v∥2 = 1,                         (3.11)

and consider the asymptotic low-SNR regime in which the noise level σ 2 and the dimension
d are fixed while β → 0. For notational brevity, write

                                   J(β) ≜ J(βv) = J(x⋆ ).                                (3.12)

In this regime, the responsibilities γℓ (x; Y ) are nearly uniform over shifts, and the Jacobian
admits a Taylor expansion in the parameter β/σ. The results established below formalize
this small-β expansion and yield an explicit leading-order contraction rate in this low SNR
regime.
    Recall that F denotes the unitary DFT matrix on Cd with columns {fk }d−1         k=0 defined
                                                                      ∗       d
in (2.1). Define the complex Fourier coefficients of v by V ≜ F v ∈ C . Since v is real,
the coefficients satisfy the conjugate symmetry V−k = Vk , for k ∈ [d]. We work under the
standard non-vanishing assumption Vk ̸= 0, for all k ̸= 0, commonly used in the MRA
literature [12, 8, 41]. For notational convenience, we assume d is odd; all statements extend
to even d with only minor modifications.

3.3    Two-phase convergence and iteration complexity
By Corollary 3.5, for each frequency pair {k, −k}, the population EM Jacobian J(β) has a
2 × 2 DFT block with orthonormal eigenvectors {uk , wk } and eigenvalues
                                                                       4
                          β2
                                       4
        ctr                      2      β           flat               β
       λuk (J(β)) = 1 − 2 2 |Vk | + O     4
                                              ,    λwk (J(β)) = 1 + O      .      (3.23)
                          σ             σ                              σ4

Hence, each block decomposes into a contracting signal-dependent direction uk with geomet-
ric contraction factor 1 − Θ(β 2 /σ 2 ) = 1 − Θ(SNR), and a flat direction wk whose gap to 1
is only O(β 4 /σ 4 ) = O(SNR2 ). The resulting local dynamics are two-phase: (i) a transient
fast phase while the error has nontrivial components along {uk }, yielding per-step shrinkage
≈ 1 − 2(β 2 /σ 2 )|Vk |2 ; (ii) a slow tail once the error mass is concentrated in the flat subspace
span{wk }, for which the asymptotic contraction factor becomes 1−O(β 4 /σ 4 ) = 1−O(SNR2 ).
Consequently, the spectral radius is governed by the flat branches (see Figure 3(c)). We sum-
marize this two-phase behavior in the following theorem, proved in Appendix D.5.

Theorem 3.6 (Two-phase convergence). Consider the setting described in Theorem 3.3 with

                                                 22
Remark 3.7 (Basin of attraction). At β = 0 (equivalently, SNR = 0), the population EM
Jacobian has eigenvalues 1 for all Fourier blocks k ̸= 0. Hence, the population map is no
longer locally contractive in any direction orthogonal to the mean, and the basin of attraction
Uβ in Theorem 3.6 cannot be taken uniformly in β as β → 0. However, for every β > 0, J(β)
admits a strict spectral gap below 1, and therefore there exists a β-dependent neighborhood
Uβ of x⋆ on which the Jacobian governs the local dynamics. All results in Sections 3-6 are
proved on such a local neighborhood.

    This theorem explains the two-phase convergence visible in Figure 1: a fast transient
driven by contracting modes, followed by a slow tail governed by the flat modes. A direct
result of Theorem 3.6 is the iteration complexity of the EM in the MRA problem, as stated
next, and proved in Appendix D.6.

Corollary 3.8 (Tail iteration complexity of EM in MRA). Consider the setting described in
Theorem 3.6. Assume that the initialization is generic in the sense that the error e(0) = x̂(0) −
x⋆ has nonzero projection onto the eigenvector of J(β) corresponding to the largest eigenvalue
ρ (J(β)) (the slowest flat eigenspace). Then, there exist constants β0 > 0 and C > 0 such
that the following holds: for all 0 < β ≤ β0 and any target accuracy εabs ∈ (0, ∥e(0) ∥2 ], any
iteration index t for which ∥e(t) ∥2 ≤ εabs must satisfy

                                        1 σ4
                                                   (0) 
                                                   ∥e ∥2
                                    t≥      4
                                              log          .                             (3.29)
                                        C β          εabs

    Corollary 3.8 shows that, in the low–SNR regime β/σ ≪ 1, the iteration complexity
is governed by the flat Fourier modes whose one-step contraction is only 1 − O(β 4 /σ 4 ) =
1−O(SNR2 ). Consequently, even when initialized inside the local basin, EM requires at least
on the order of (σ 4 /β 4 ) ≍ SNR−2 iterations to reduce the error to a fixed tolerance. This
slow rate creates a pronounced computational bottleneck: as SNR decreases, the number of
iterations (and hence total runtime) increases at least quadratically in SNR−1 .

3.4    Fourier magnitudes and phases at low SNR
The next result refines the picture obtained in Corollary 3.5 and Theorem 3.6 by working
directly in the Fourier basis and decomposing, on each nonzero block {k, −k}, the error into
radial and tangential components, that is, into errors in the Fourier magnitudes and phases.

Proposition 3.9 (Local expansion of Fourier magnitudes/phases around x⋆ ). Consider the
setting of Theorem 3.3 and Corollary 3.5. Let x⋆ denote the ground truth and let x be any
signal in Rd . Let F be the unitary DFT matrix and define X⋆ ≜ F ∗ x⋆ and X ≜ F ∗ x. Assume
                                                             (d−1)/2
that X⋆ [k] ̸= 0 for all 1 ≤ k ≤ (d − 1)/2. Let {uk , wk }k=1        be the eigenvectors from
Corollary 3.5 and set e ≜ x − x⋆ .
    Then, there exist constants r0 > 0 and C > 0, depending only on x⋆ and d, such that
whenever ∥e∥2 ≤ r0 , the following first-order expansions hold for every k = 1, . . . , (d − 1)/2:
                                                     1
        (Magnitude error)       |X[k]| − |X⋆ [k]| = √ ⟨e, uk ⟩ + Rkmag (e),                (3.30)
                                                      2


                                               23
                                                                      1
               (Phase error)        ϕX [k] − ϕX⋆ [k] = − √                   ⟨e, wk ⟩ + Rkph (e),   (3.31)
                                                                        ⋆
                                                                   2 |X [k]|

where the remainder terms satisfy the uniform bound

                                          |Rkmag (e)| + |Rkph (e)| ≤ C ∥e∥22 .
                                                                  
                                max                                                                 (3.32)
                            1≤k≤(d−1)/2


    Proposition 3.9 (proved in Appendix D.7) may be viewed as a Fourier-coordinate refine-
ment of the two-phase convergence result in Theorem 3.6. On each nonzero block {k, −k},
the eigenvector uk is aligned, to first order, with the radial direction in the complex plane: the
magnitude error |X̂(t) [k]| − |X⋆ [k]| is a fixed multiple of ⟨e(t) , uk ⟩ up to O(∥e(t) ∥22 ) corrections.
Thus, the eigenvalue λuk = 1 − O(SNR), defined in (3.23), governs the leading-order decay
of the magnitude error. In contrast, wk spans the tangential direction corresponding to in-
finitesimal rotations of X̂(t) [k], and Proposition 3.9 shows that the phase error ϕX̂(t) [k]−ϕX⋆ [k]
is controlled, to first order, by ⟨e(t) , wk ⟩, again up to O(∥e(t) ∥22 ) terms. Its eigenvalue satisfies
λwk = 1 − O(SNR2 ), so any contraction of the phase error can occur, at best, on the much
slower SNR2 scale at the level of the Jacobian.
    This first-order dichotomy is consistent with the classical moment-based identifiability
picture in MRA. In the Fourier domain, the second-order autocorrelation (equivalently, the
power spectrum) determines the Fourier magnitudes {|X⋆ [k]|2 } but contains no phase infor-
mation. Accordingly, in the low-SNR expansion, the magnitude (radial) directions already
appear in the EM linearization at order O(SNR), leading to a comparatively faster contrac-
tion. In contrast, identifying the phases requires genuinely third-order information, such as
the bispectrum [12, 8, 41]; correspondingly, the phase (tangential) directions enter the EM
linearization only through higher-order terms in SNR, and thus can contract only on a much
slower scale. This result is in line with the result established in [26].


4     EM bias to initialization at low SNR
In contrast to the previous section, which studied convergence to the ground truth once the
iterate enters a basin of attraction, here we focus on the complementary phenomenon of bias
to initialization in the population low-SNR limit. In this regime, both the true signal and
the initialization are small relative to the noise level, and as a result the population EM
dynamics exhibit only a very weak per-iteration drift away from the initialization.
    We implement the low-SNR limit via the scaling

                               x⋆ = βv,        x̂(t) = βv̂ (t) ,        β → 0,                       (4.1)

and assume that the normalized ground truth and initialization are uniformly bounded,
∥v∥ ≤ P , and ∥v̂ (0) ∥ ≤ P , for some fixed constant P < ∞ (independent of β). To state the
result, let 1 ∈ Rd denote the all-ones vector and define the mean projector Πmean ≜ d1 11⊤ ,
so that Πmean x is the constant vector whose entries equal the sample mean d1 dj=1 xj .

   Theorem 4.1, proved in Appendix E.2, formalizes an intrinsic iteration-complexity barrier
underlying the bias to initialization in low SNR. The population EM normalized drift in
the non-mean components is bounded by O(SNR) per iteration. Thus, a constant-fraction
displacement from a generic initialization requires at least T ≳ SNR−1 iterations.


5      Einstein from Noise at vanishing SNR
In this section, we analyze the Einstein from Noise phenomenon, extending previous analyses
from the single-iteration hard-assignment algorithm [7] to the more general setting of multiple
iterations in the EM algorithm.
    The original Einstein from Noise phenomenon illustrates a striking instance of model bias
in scientific data analysis. Consider the following scenario: researchers aim to reconstruct
an underlying signal based on the assumption that each observation is a noisy, randomly
shifted copy of a known template. The phenomenon was first demonstrated using an image
of Einstein as the template, which gave rise to its name [50, 24]. However, in reality, the
observations are purely noise and do not contain a signal. This corresponds to the extreme
case of SNR = 0, where the data no longer follow the MRA model described in (1.1), but
instead adhere to the distribution:
                                                     i.i.d.
                                   y1 , y2 , . . . , yn ∼ N (0, σ 2 Id ).                      (5.1)

Despite the absence of a signal, researchers, who mistakenly believe the data contain mean-
ingful information (i.e., an image of Einstein), may still apply standard hard-assignment
methods, such as aligning observations via cross-correlation with the template and aver-
aging the aligned outputs. Interestingly, empirical results have shown that when the data


                                                     25
is pure noise, the reconstructed image resembles the initial template (e.g., Einstein’s im-
age) [50, 51]. This effect, where structure is seemingly recovered from pure noise, is not just
a theoretical phenomenon, but has played a prominent role in real-world scientific debates,
most notably the structural analysis of an HIV molecule [34, 24, 61, 57, 33], as recently
revisited in [7]. Here, we extend this analysis beyond the single-step hard-assignment setting
to understand how the iterative EM algorithm interacts with this form of confirmation bias
in the absence of a true signal.
Summary of prior work. Previous studies investigated the behavior of the Einstein from
Noise phenomenon in the context of the hard-assignment estimator. It was shown that in the
asymptotic regime, as n → ∞, the Fourier phases of the estimator converge to those of the
template [7]. Specifically, the MSE between the template’s and the estimator’s Fourier phases
decays at a rate of 1/n. Since Fourier phases are key to defining geometric structures like
edges and contours, this explains why the reconstructed image retains structural similarities
to the template. In high-dimensional settings, where the signal dimension increases, the rate
of convergence of the Fourier phases was found to be inversely proportional to the square
of the template’s Fourier magnitudes. Additionally, in this regime, the Fourier magnitudes
of the estimator converge to a scaled version of the template’s Fourier magnitudes. Similar
results were extended to Gaussian mixture models, showing that the initialization of the
centroids in algorithms such as K-means and EM can induce confirmation bias [6]. See [52]
for a related recent result along similar lines.
Notations. For clarity of notation, and since the observations contain no signal, we denote
them as yi = ξi for all i ∈ {1, 2, . . . , n}, where ξi ∼ N (0, σ 2 Id ) represents isotropic Gaussian
noise, according to (5.1). To further simplify the exposition, we assume throughout this
section that the noise variance is σ 2 = 1.
    Let X̂(t) , and Ni , denote the unitary DFTs of x̂(t) , and ξi , respectively, for i ∈ {1, 2, . . . , n}.
These DFT sequences can be equivalently represented in polar coordinates as follows,

                      X̂(t) = {|X̂(t) [k]|ejϕX̂(t) [k] }d−1
                                                        k=0 ,   Ni = {|Ni [k]| ejϕNi [k] }d−1
                                                                                          k=0 .        (5.2)
                                                     d/2                  d/2
Note that the random variables {|Ni [k]|}k=0 and {ϕNi [k]}k=0 are two independent sequences
of i.i.d. random variables, such that, |Ni [k]| ∼ Rayleigh (σ 2 ) has Rayleigh distribution, and
the phase ϕNi [k] ∼ Unif[−π, π) is uniformly distributed over [−π, π).
    In this section, we analyze the behavior of Einstein from Noise in the population limit,
where n → ∞, so all quantities are understood in this limit, and the EM update is deter-
ministic (i.e., there is no sampling randomness). The finite-sample analysis can be found
in Section 6.4. We begin with the finite-dimensional setting (fixed d), and then treat the
high-dimensional regime (where d → ∞) in Section 5.2.

5.1     Fixed dimension: phase invariance and magnitude decay
We start with analyzing the Einstein from Noise population EM for successive iterations.

the population EM operator M associated with the model (5.1), obtained in the limit n → ∞.

    Theorem 5.1 (proved in Appendix K.1) shows that, at the population level and for fixed d,

      In particular, x̂(t) → 0 as t → ∞.

   Corollary 5.2 highlights a distinctive feature of the Einstein from Noise regime: inde-
pendently of the initialization, the population EM trajectory converges to 0, while retaining
the initialization phases. However, the contraction towards zero becomes extremely weak
near the origin (as shown in Figure 2(b)), which motivates the statement below (proved in
Appendix K.3).

                                                  27
Theorem 5.3 (Low-magnitude regime: contraction expansion and global rate). Assume
the conditions of Corollary 5.2, and suppose that the initialization is in the low-magnitude
region: ∥x̂(0) ∥ ≤ δ, where δ > 0 is sufficiently small. Then:
  1. (Successive-step expansion.) For every t ≥ 0 and every 1 ≤ k ≤ d − 1,
                                           (t)

   The bounds (5.9) imply the asymptotic decay

uniformly over all iteration counts T as the initialization magnitude tends to zero. Thus,
the EM
     √ iterates produces a convergence of the Fourier magnitudes toward 0 in a slow rate
of 1/ T , while leaving phases unchanged.
Comparison with geometric convergence near the ground truth. The bounds
in (5.9) exhibit a qualitatively different convergence mechanism from the Jacobian-based
analysis around a nonzero ground truth, as analyzed in Section 3. When x⋆ = βv with
β > 0, the Jacobian expansion in Corollary 5.2 shows that the population EM map is lo-
cally contractive around x⋆ so that, as long as the iterates remain in a sufficiently small
neighborhood of x⋆ , the error decays geometrically at rate ρ(J(x⋆ ))t . In the Einstein from
Noise settings, where SNR = 0, the Jacobian is flat in the sense that ρ(J(0)) = 1 and the
first nontrivial term in the Taylor expansion of the EM map is quadratic in the Fourier
magnitudes, as captured by the expansion (5.8). Consequently, the linear term provides no
contraction, and the dynamics are governed by the second-order term only, which integrates
into the bound (5.9).

   Theorem 5.4(1) shows that, under asymptotic decorrelation of distant shifts, each pop-
ulation update becomes increasingly close to the identity as d → ∞. In particular, the
                                                                              (t)
contraction factors from the finite-dimensional analysis (see (5.3)) satisfy αd,k → 1 for ev-
                                                                             (t)
ery fixed Fourier mode k, so the Fourier magnitudes of x̂d remain essentially those of the
initialization over any fixed number of iterations. As a direct consequence, Theorem 5.4(2)
shows a high-dimensional stagnation phenomenon, in which the population EM trajectory
does not move appreciably away from its initialization over any finite number of iterations.
Thus, unlike the finite-dimensional Einstein from Noise behavior of Corollary 5.2, the high-
dimensional population dynamics do not exhibit perceptible magnitude convergence to 0.


6      Finite-sample analysis
This section develops a complementary, finite-sample analysis of EM. Throughout this sec-
                                                                    (t)
tion, we use the notation x̂(t) for the empirical EM iterates and x̂pop for the corresponding
population iterates initialized at the same point. That is, for a fixed number of iterations
T ∈ N, and for t = 0, 1, . . . , T − 1,

                x̂(0)     (0)
                                     x̂(t+1)     (t)
                                                        x̂(t+1) = Mn x̂(t) ; Y .
                                                                             
                  pop = x̂ ,           pop = M x̂pop ,                                   (6.1)



                                                      29
                                                     (t)
Our objective is to control the deviation ∥x̂(t) − x̂pop ∥ uniformly over t ≤ T , and to translate
such bounds into quantitative statements about finite-sample effects on the EM trajectory
in the regimes studied in this work.
    Section 6.1 provides sufficient conditions under which the empirical EM operator Mn
tracks its population counterpart M : under local contraction and a uniform deviation
bound, the empirical iterates follow the population trajectory geometrically up to a sta-
tistical neighborhood. Section 6.2 then shows how this same finite-sample perturbation can
qualitatively reshape the dynamics at moderately low SNR, producing the Ghost of New-
ton phenomenon: the iterates initially improve by tracking the contracting population map,
but after a crossover time the accumulated deviation dominates and the trajectory peels
away toward a sample-dependent fixed point. Section 6.3 complements this with a necessary
perspective, proving that even inside the local basin there is an intrinsic finite-sample error
floor for any empirical fixed point. Finally, Section 6.4 turns to the SNR = 0 setting and
quantifies how finite-sample fluctuations manifest as a per-iteration n−1 Fourier-phase drift
and its accumulation across iterations.

6.1    Finite-sample EM tracking
The following theorem formalizes the finite-sample tracking behavior of EM in a local basin
of attraction under standard regularity conditions. It adapts a classical result for Gaussian
mixture models (GMMs) to the MRA setting [4]. For a map M : Rd → Rd and a set S ⊆ Rd ,
we denote the image of S under M by M (S) ≜ {M (x) : x ∈ S}.
Assumption 6.1 (Finite-sample EM tracking conditions). Let M and Mn (·; Y) be the pop-
ulation and empirical EM operators defined in (2.15) and (2.13). Fix a compact set B ⊂ Rd
containing the ground-truth x⋆ , such that M (x⋆ ) = x⋆ . Assume that the following hold:

  1. (A1) Local contraction of the population map. There exists κ ∈ [0, 1) such that for all
     x ∈ B,

                                     ∥M (x) − x⋆ ∥ ≤ κ ∥x − x⋆ ∥.                           (6.2)

  2. (A2) Uniform finite-sample accuracy. For every sample size n ∈ N and confidence level
     δ ∈ (0, 1), there exists a deterministic tolerance εM (n, δ) > 0 such that
                                                                 
                          P sup ∥Mn (x; Y) − M (x)∥ ≤ εM (n, δ) ≥ 1 − δ.            (6.3)
                             x∈B


  3. (A3) Initialization and invariance. The initialization satisfies x̂(0) ∈ B, and on the
     event in (6.3) the set B is invariant under both maps:

                                   M (B) ⊆ B,        Mn (B; Y) ⊆ B.                         (6.4)

   Assumption 6.1 postulates a local basin B that is invariant under both the population
map M and the empirical map Mn , and assumes that M is contractive on B. In practice,
the contraction factor κ may be chosen using the spectral-radius bound on the Jacobian

                                                30
J(x⋆ ) from Theorem 3.3. The tolerance εM (n, δ) is the deterministic quantity appearing
in (6.3) that upper-bounds, with probability at least 1 − δ, the uniform deviation of the
empirical operator from its population counterpart over the basin. Under these conditions,
the empirical EM iterates follow the population iterates at a geometric rate until they reach
a neighborhood whose radius is proportional to εM (n, δ).
Theorem 6.2 (Finite-sample EM tracking). Recall the definition of the empirical EM iter-
ates (6.1). Then, under Assumption 6.1, with probability at least 1 − δ,
                                                              1 − κt
                         ∥x̂(t) − x⋆ ∥ ≤ κt ∥x̂(0) − x⋆ ∥ +          εM (n, δ).              (6.5)
                                                              1−κ
In particular, the asymptotic tracking bias satisfies
                                                           εM (n, δ)
                                 lim sup ∥x̂(t) − x⋆ ∥ ≤             .                       (6.6)
                                   t→∞                      1−κ
    Theorem 6.2 shows that the gap between the empirical and population EM trajectories
is governed by εM (n, δ). To make this quantitative, we next bound εM (n, δ) for MRA
observations (1.1); the proof appears in Appendix G.2.
Proposition 6.3 (Uniform sample-population deviation). Let Y = {yi }ni=1 be the observa-
tions drawn according to the MRA model (1.1), with a ground-truth signal x⋆ ∈√Rd . Fix a
compact ball for the parameter space B = {x : ∥x∥2 ≤ R} and assume ∥x⋆ ∥ ≲ σ d. Then,
there exist universal constants C0 , C1 > 0 such that for every δ ∈ (0, 1), if n ≥ C0 (log(2/δ))3 ,
then with probability at least 1 − δ,

6.2    Ghost of Newton: finite-sample artifacts
At moderately low SNR, we observe an unexpected finite-sample artifact in the EM dy-
namics. Figure 4 illustrates the effect. Specifically, Figure 4(a) plots the reconstruction
MSE (defined in (2.4) with respect to the ground truth) as a function of the iteration index
t. Starting from the initial template, the EM iterates first move towards the true signal,
yielding a clear initial decrease in error. After a transient period, however, the trajectory re-
verses direction: the error begins to increase and the estimate gradually drifts away from the
ground truth. We emphasize that this behavior is not tied to the specific Newton/Einstein
pair used in the running example; analogous non-monotone error curves appear for other
ground-truth signals and various initial templates. We adopt the term “Ghost of Newton”
for consistency with the illustrative example in Figure 4.
    Empirically, this drift-after-improvement regime is specific to EM. The hard-assignment
algorithm does not exhibit a comparable intermediate phase; instead, as the SNR increases, it
undergoes a sharp transition from the Einstein from Noise behavior (template reconstruction
at very low SNR) to accurate recovery of the ground truth, without passing through a gradual
deterioration stage. Additional numerical evidence and parameter sweeps supporting this
distinction are provided in Appendix N.2.

Figure 4: The Ghost of Newton phenomenon. (a) MSE, as defined in (2.4), between the
EM estimator at iteration t and the true signal x⋆ , plotted as a function of iteration t. Initially,
the algorithm converges toward the true signal of Newton, but after several iterations, it begins to
diverge, ultimately yielding a noisy reconstruction. (b) The EM update consists of two components:
                        (t+1)                           (t+1)
the aligned signal, x̂signal , and the aligned noise, x̂noise . In the Ghost of Newton regime, the aligned
noise, despite originating from pure noise, resembles a noisy version of the Newton image. This
misleading signal steers the algorithm away from the ground truth. (c) Visualization of the aligned
         (t+1)                    (t+1)
signal x̂signal , aligned noise x̂noise , and the total estimate x̂(t+1) at representative iterations. In
the early stages, the aligned noise and the estimate resemble the initial template of Einstein. As
the iterations progress, the Newton image gradually emerges. However, after approximately 50
iterations, the influence of the aligned noise dominates, steering the reconstruction away from
Newton and resulting in a noisy and degraded estimate. The simulation corresponds to a fixed
signal-to-noise ratio of SNR = 5 × 10−3 , with an image size of d = 64 × 64 and n = 2 × 104
observations.


Finite-sample artifact. The empirical EM map Mn is a small random perturbation of its
deterministic population counterpart M . While population EM converges to x⋆ whenever
initialized in its basin of attraction, the perturbed map Mn converges instead to a sample-


                                                                           32
dependent fixed point x̂⋆n = Mn (x̂⋆n ; Y) that maximizes the empirical log-likelihood. Thus, if
x̂(0) lies in the population basin, the empirical iterates initially track the contracting popula-
tion dynamics, but eventually peel away as the small per-iteration perturbations accumulate,
drifting toward x̂⋆n .
     Formally, let x̂(t+1) = Mn (x̂(t) ; Y) denote the empirical EM iterates. Under the tracking
guarantee of Theorem 6.2, the group-invariant error satisfies, for all t ≥ 0,

                                                              1 − κt
                        ∥x̂(t) − x⋆ ∥ ≤ κt ∥x̂(0) − x⋆ ∥ +           εM (n, δ),                 (6.8)
                                                              1−κ
where the first term captures population contraction and the second term reflects the am-
plified finite-sample discrepancy between Mn and M . Consequently, the Ghost-of-Newton
regime begins with the population term dominating, but after sufficiently many iterations
the finite-sample term overtakes it, producing a rebound away from the truth.
    Let e0 ≜ ∥x̂(0) − x⋆ ∥ and define teq as the iteration index where the two terms in (6.8)
balance:
                                                 1 − κteq
                                     κteq e0 =            εM (n, δ).                            (6.9)
                                                  1−κ

   Since εM (n, δ) ≍ n−1/2 by (6.7), increasing the number of observations simultaneously
delays the rebound (through a larger teq in (6.10)) and lowers the eventual error floor ε∞ ≍
εM /(1 − κ). Conversely, decreasing the SNR increases κ toward 1, weakening population
contraction, reducing − log(κ), and thereby advancing the rebound while inflating the floor
by the factor (1 − κ)−1 .
Empirical demonstration. Figures 5 demonstrate that the Ghost of Newton behavior
is governed by the number of observations n and the SNR. Equation (6.11)     √ predicts that,
in the finite-sample-dominated regime, the crossover time satisfies teq ∝ n, in agreement
with the empirical scalings in Figures 5(a-b). A striking additional signature is that the
iteration at which the MSE begins to increase coincides with a sharp rise in the empirical
log-likelihood; this alignment persists across SNR levels and sample sizes. Crucially, although
EM monotonically increases the log-likelihood at every step [32], likelihood ascent alone does
not guarantee monotone improvement in group-invariant MSE relative to the ground truth.

Figure 5: The Impact of the number of observations and SNR on the Ghost of Newton
phenomenon. (a, b) Effect of increasing the number of observations at a fixed SNR. Parameters:
image size d = 64×64, SNR = 5×10−3 . A larger number of observations reduces the impact of noise
and mitigates the Ghost of Newton effect. (c, d) Effect of increasing the SNR at a fixed number

transition is indicated by dashed lines. Notably, this pattern consistently appears across different
numbers of observations and SNR levels.


6.3                   Sample complexity threshold for EM
Next, we turn to necessary sample-size requirements for EM in MRA. Up to this point, we
have derived sufficient conditions under which the empirical EM operator Mn closely tracks
its population counterpart M , and hence inherits its local contraction behavior. Here we
prove a converse statement: under mild regularity assumptions (listed in Appendix G.3 and
verified for the MRA model), finite-sample fluctuations prevent any empirical fixed point
inside the local basin from approaching x⋆ beyond the intrinsic statistical noise floor.

Theorem 6.4 (Necessary sample complexity of EM). Consider the MRA model in the low-

                                                                                  34
SNR regime x⋆ = βv, ∥v∥2 = 1, with fixed σ 2 > 0 and varying 0 < β ≪ σ. Fix any target
absolute accuracy εabs ∈ (0, R0 ], where R0 > 0 is chosen so that the Euclidean ball B(x⋆ , R0 )
lies within the local neighborhood U guaranteed by Theorem 3.3. Then, there exists a constant
Cnec > 0 (depending only on the normalized spectrum of v and on d, but not on n, β, σ) such
that, with constant positive probability, any empirical fixed point x̂⋆n = Mn (x̂⋆n ; Y) lying in a
sufficiently small basin of x⋆ and satisfying
                                         ∥x̂⋆n − x⋆ ∥2
                                                       ≤ εabs                                   (6.12)
                                             ∥x⋆ ∥2
must obey the necessary sample-size lower bound
                                                     σ6 1
                                          n ≥ Cnec             .                                (6.13)
                                                     β 6 ε2abs
   The proof of Theorem 6.4 appears in Appendix G.5. The necessary condition (6.13) can
be rewritten as
                                          n ≳ SNR−3 ε−2
                                                     abs .                                      (6.14)
Thus, even within the best-case local basin where the population EM map is contractive,
finite-sample fluctuations impose an intrinsic statistical floor: achieving relative accuracy εabs
requires on the order of SNR−3 samples. In particular, Theorem 6.4 matches the information-
theoretic SNR−3 scaling [8].

6.4     Finite-sample Einstein from Noise: phase drift
We now turn to the finite-sample Einstein from Noise regime, focusing on the drift of Fourier
phases induced by finite-sample fluctuations. Recall the Fourier-domain notation introduced
in (5.2): we write {X̂(t) [k]}d−1
                              k=0 for DFT coefficients of the empirical EM iterate x̂ , and
                                                                                               (t)
         d−1
{N̂i [k]}k=0 for the DFT coefficients of the noise vector ξi , for i = {1, 2, . . . n}. We further use
the polar representation X̂(t) [k] = |X̂(t) [k]| eiϕX̂(t) [k] to denote Fourier magnitudes and phases.
Throughout this subsection, we use the term MSE to refer to the initialization-discrepancy
error, namely, the expected squared difference between the Fourier phases of the iterate and
those of the initialization. Specifically, for a frequency k and iteration t, we define

Fourier-phases drift: Single iteration. Figure 6 illustrates the finite-sample bias after
a single Einstein from Noise step, comparing the hard-assignment update (Algorithm 2) and
the EM update (Algorithm 1). The upper panels show the appearance of structural similarity
to initialization as the number of observations n increases. The lower panels quantify this
effect through the MSE of the Fourier phases (6.15) between successive iterates. Empirically,
the phase MSE decays inversely proportional to the number of observations at a rate n−1
for both algorithms, with stronger Fourier modes converging faster. Notably, EM exhibits a
larger bias toward the template than hard assignment, as reflected both visually and in the
phase-MSE curves.
    These trends are consistent with the finite-sample guaranties developed in the following
for EM; the corresponding single-iteration analysis for hard assignment appears in [7].

    Proposition 6.5, proved in Appendix M.3, characterizes the one-step finite-sample phase
drift at frequency k. In particular, conditional on the current iterate x̂(t) , the per-iteration
phase mean-squared error is of order 1/n, with leading term Ck (x̂(t) )/n. Next, we specialize
this constant to the low-magnitude scaling regime of Theorem 5.3 and derive an explicit
approximation for Ck (x̂(t) ) in that setting.

Proposition 6.6 (Low-magnitude scaling of Ck (x̂(t) )). Fix a finite iteration T ∈ N, a con-
fidence level δ ∈ (0, 1), and an index t ∈ {0, . . . , T − 1}. Let {x̂(t) }Tt=0 be the empirical EM

                                                                                  36
iterates defined in (6.1). Assume in addition that x̂(t) lies in the low-magnitude regime of
Theorem 5.3. Then,
                                       Ck (x̂(t) ) = Θ(∥x̂(t) ∥2 ).                            (6.17)
Moreover, if the sample size is large enough so that n ≳ ∥x̂(t) ∥−6 log(1/δ), then with proba-
bility at least 1 − δ,
                                Ck (x̂(t+1) ) − Ck (x̂(t) ) ≤ c ∥x̂(t) ∥4 ,                    (6.18)
where the probability is with respect to the randomness in forming the next empirical update
x̂(t+1) , given the current iterate x̂(t) , and c is a constant independent of ∥x̂(t) ∥.
    Proposition 6.6, proved in Appendix M.4, describes the behavior of the one-step phase-
drift constant in the low-magnitude regime. When ∥x̂(t) ∥ is small, it yields the envelope
estimate Ck (x̂(t) ) = Θ(∥x̂(t) ∥2 ). In particular, E|∆ϕ(t) [k]|2 = Θ(∥x̂(t) ∥2 /n). Moreover, the
slow-variation bound in (6.18) implies that the constants Ck (x̂(t) ) change only gradually
across iterations; that is, the phase-drift rate remains essentially stable over a moderate
number of steps while the iterate magnitude stays small.
Fourier-phases drift: Multi-iteration. In the population limit, Proposition 5.2 shows
that the EM phases remain identical to those of the initialization along the entire trajectory.
However, at finite n, each iteration introduces a small random phase drift, which accumu-
lates over time. Figure 7 demonstrates this multi-iteration behavior. Panel (a) plots the
Pearson cross-correlation (PCC) between the estimate at iteration t and the initialization;
the correlation increases with n, indicating a strengthening bias toward the template as
more observations are available. Panel (b) shows the Fourier phase MSE (6.15) between the
estimate and the template, which empirically scales as ∝ t2 /n over the transient regime.
    We now formalize the accumulation of finite-sample phase errors across iterations, which
is proved in Appendix M.5.

let {yi }ni=1 follow the model (5.1). Let x̂(t) be the EM iterates from Algorithm 1, with DFT
