Hence, as long as T ≪ ∥x̂(T ) ∥−2 , the cumulative phase MSE scales as ∝ T 2 /n, matching
Figure 7(b). This provides a quantitative mechanism for the persistence of initialization bias
in large low-SNR datasets.
    As the Fourier-phase MSE scales as 1/n, Appendix N explores mitigation strategies based
on mini-batching, where each iteration uses only a smaller subset of n observations. Our
results show that such mini-batching schemes reduce sensitivity to initialization and improve
computational efficiency, while achieving reconstruction accuracy comparable to full-batch
EM.


7                                     Discussion and outlook
7.1                                    Extension to additional group actions
Although our analysis focuses on the classical MRA model (1.1) with a cyclic-shift group
action, the structural features revealed by our study of the EM dynamics suggest that similar
phenomena may extend to more general latent group actions. In a general MRA formula-
tion, one considers a compact group G acting continuously and orthogonally on a finite-
dimensional real vector space V , with an unknown signal x ∈ V and observations

                                                             (General MRA model)                             yi = gi · x + ξi ,                    gi ∈ G,                      (7.1)

                                                                                           38
for i ∈ {0, . . . , n − 1}, where ξi are i.i.d. noise terms and (g · x)(w) ≜ x(g −1 w). In this
setting, the natural goal is to recover the G-orbit of x from the noisy samples {yi }ni=1 .
    It is useful to distinguish which aspects of our analysis rely on the special structure of
cyclic shifts, namely that the acting group is discrete and abelian, and which reflect more
general features of EM under group actions. The finite-sample bounds and the Ghost of
Newton phenomenon rely primarily on the unitarity of the group action and on the resulting
covariance structure of the per-sample EM update. We therefore expect these aspects of the
theory to extend, with suitable technical modifications, to general MRA models in which G
acts unitarily on V . By contrast, the explicit second-order expansion of the Jacobian and
its block-diagonal Fourier structure make essential use of the commutative representation
theory of the cyclic group. Extending these spectral calculations to more general compact
groups, especially non-abelian groups, such as SO(n) for n > 2, would both broaden the
range of applications and clarify which geometric features of the group control the local
contraction properties of EM.
    We also conjecture that the two-phase convergence behavior established in Section 3 is
not specific to the cyclic-shift MRA model (1.1). Heuristically, the initial fast phase reflects
contraction along magnitude-like directions that are identifiable from low-order invariants,
whereas the slow tail is governed by nearly flat directions corresponding to phase-like de-
grees of freedom. Making this intuition precise for general compact groups would require
identifying the appropriate analog of Fourier magnitudes and phases, which is closely related
to the decomposition of V into irreducible representations, and tracking EM on each com-
ponent. For SO(n), for example, natural analogs of these quantities are the second-order
invariants (Gram matrices of the spherical harmonic coefficients [13]) and the third-order
invariants (bispectrum) [14], which respectively play the roles of magnitude-and phase-type
information. Such a theory could lead to a general iteration-complexity bounds and explain
why certain representation components dominate the asymptotic behavior.
    Our analysis of the Einstein from Noise phenomenon in the cyclic-shift MRA model
suggests a broader mechanism of algorithmic confirmation bias in latent group-action models,
which is also supported by classical results in the cryo-EM literature [24]. In the noise-only
setting, where no signal present, the population EM dynamics drive the Fourier magnitudes
toward zero while leaving the Fourier phases equal to those of the initialization, so the
iterate retains a persistent imprint of the initial template. More generally, we conjecture that
for other group-action models there exist analogous nearly invariant directions that remain
stable over many iterations and likewise preserve memory of the initialization. Characterizing
these invariant directions for general compact groups, and relating them to the hierarchy of
invariant features required to identify the orbit, may provide a general principle for predicting
when and how EM exhibits confirmation bias toward its starting point.

7.2    Mitigation strategies
Mitigation strategies of the Ghost of Newton phenomenon. Addressing the Ghost
of Newton phenomenon remains a challenging open problem with practical implications.
One potential solution is to apply early termination to the EM algorithm. However, this
approach is difficult to implement reliably because the optimal stopping point depends on
both the SNR and the number of observations, making it challenging to generalize across

                                               39
different scenarios. One possible approach is to apply change point detection methods [2] in
combination with gap statistics [58].
Beyond EM: second-order and acceleration schemes. A natural question is whether
second-order or acceleration techniques can mitigate the slow-convergence bottleneck we
identify for EM in the low-SNR regime, both analytically and numerically. There are many
plausible variants to explore, including Newton–Raphson and quasi-Newton methods [38,
36], as well as accelerated EM schemes based on extrapolation or fixed-point acceleration
(e.g., Anderson-type) [63, 69], and momentum-based first-order methods (e.g., Nesterov
acceleration) [37]. A systematic study of these alternatives, however, is beyond the scope of
the present paper and merits dedicated investigation. That said, our analysis suggests that
the bottleneck is rooted in the local geometry of the objective EM map in low SNR (e.g.,
near-flat directions of the Jacobian), and thus we expect that purely first-order schemes may
continue to exhibit a similar slowdown.

7.3    Comparison with the method of moments
Our discouraging findings for EM in the low-SNR regime suggest considering alternative
estimation approaches, such as the method of moments. A key distinction is that several
limitations of EM persist even at the population level and therefore do not vanish with more
observations; for instance, the slow convergence phenomenon in low SNR holds regardless
of the sample size. In contrast, method of moments can leverage additional data directly:
by collecting more samples one can estimate the relevant moments with increasing accuracy,
and in the limit obtain essentially exact moment estimates.
    The main drawback of the method of moments is computational rather than statistical,
namely the challenge of inverting the (typically nonlinear) moment equations to recover
the underlying signal. For the MRA models considered in this paper, however, provable
inversion algorithms are available; see, e.g., [12, 41], and further extensions to more general
group actions such as SO(3) and SE(3) in [14, 5].


Data Availability
The detailed implementation and code are available at https://github.com/AmnonBa/EM-
MRA-low-SNR.

                                                45
Appendix
Appendix organization. This appendix is organized as follows. Appendix A provides
foundational background, including a derivation of the likelihood function for the MRA
model, the EM update rule, and a brief overview of prior theoretical work relevant to our
setting. Appendix B introduces the nonlinear dynamical systems framework and collects
general properties that are later applied to the EM population map; these are used in
Appendix C, where we establish the local spectral properties and convergence behavior of
the population EM operator. In Appendix D we derive the population EM Jacobian in the
low-SNR regime and prove the associated statements, including the two-phase convergence
behavior and the tail iteration complexity. Appendix E provides the proof of the bias-to-
initialization property (Theorem 4.1).
    Appendix F presents the preliminaries for the finite-sample analysis, relying on tools
from empirical process theory, and Appendix G contains the corresponding finite-sample
convergence results. Appendix H focuses on the high-SNR regime, while Appendix I provides
approximation formulas and expansions tailored to the low-SNR regime.
    Finally, Appendix J introduces the framework and auxiliary lemmas for the Einstein from
Noise phenomenon. Appendix K contains the proofs for the finite-dimensional Einstein from
Noise results, Appendix L addresses the high-dimensional case, and Appendix M establishes
the corresponding finite-sample guarantees. Appendix N concludes by presenting a mitiga-
tion strategy for Einstein from Noise based on mini-batch EM and adaptive optimization.


A      Preliminaries

The EM update rule. We use the EM algorithm to compute soft assignments. EM is
widely used for Gaussian mixture models (GMMs) [20], and has been adapted for the MRA
setting [51, 49]. The EM update at iteration t is given by,

                 x̂(t+1) = arg max ETℓ1 ,...,Tℓn ∼p(·|Y,x̂(t) ) [log p (Y, {Tℓi }ni=1 | x)] .       (A.7)
                                  x∈Rd

as in (2.6). We note that the derivation for the cyclic group actions Zd presented here can

A.2     The sample complexity of the MRA model
The sample complexity of the MRA problem quantifies how the number of observations
n must scale with the noise variance σ 2 in order to reliably recover the underlying signal.
Figure 2(b) summarizes the different reconstruction regimes as a function of the SNR. In the
high-SNR regime, direct observation alignment is feasible, enabling accurate alignment and
averaging. The sample complexity in this setting is similar to that of incoherent integration,
where the underlying group actions (e.g., shifts or rotations) are assumed to be known.
    As the SNR decreases, however, alignment becomes increasingly unreliable, and the sam-
ple complexity required for accurate recovery grows rapidly. In the asymptotic regime where
both the number of samples n → ∞ and the noise variance σ 2 → ∞, recent theoretical
analyses show that a necessary condition for unique recovery is
                                             n = ω(σ 6 ),                              (A.15)
as established in [41, 8, 12]. The notation n = ω(σ 6 ) means that n/σ 6 → ∞, implying
that the number of observations must grow faster than σ 6 for consistent estimation of the
signal. This scaling law characterizes an information-theoretic lower bound that applies
to any estimator, regardless of computational constraints. In particular, the authors in [12]
provide an algorithm that uses this estimation rate, thus providing a matching upper bound.
These results are derived for the maximum-likelihood objective in (2.7), and need not apply
to alternative formulations such as the hard-assignment objective in (2.9).


B     Preliminaries: Nonlinear dynamical systems
To analyze the local convergence properties of the population EM iteration in the MRA
problem, we first provide several basic facts from the theory of nonlinear dynamical systems
near a fixed point. Throughout this appendix we work in a general deterministic setting: all
results apply to an arbitrary nonlinear fixed-point iteration with a symmetric Jacobian, and
in particular to the population EM operator considered below.

                                                   48
B.1     Local linearization and contraction
Let M : Rd → Rd be C 2 in a neighborhood of a fixed point x⋆ , with M (x⋆ ) = x⋆ . We
consider the associated fixed-point iteration x̂(t+1) = M (x̂(t) ), for t = 0, 1, . . . , and define the
error sequence

                                      et ≜ x̂(t) − x⋆ ,        t ≥ 0.                             (B.1)

Thus, by the definition of et ,

                                      et+1 = M (x⋆ + et ) − x⋆ .                                  (B.2)

In addition, by Taylor’s theorem with integral remainder, there exist constants c > 0 and
r0 > 0 such that the errors satisfy

                                          et+1 = Jet + R(et ),                                    (B.3)

where J ≜ JM (x⋆ ) denotes the Jacobian of M in x⋆ (see, Definition 3.1), and the remainder
R : Rd → Rd satisfies the quadratic bound

                              ∥R(e)∥2 ≤ c∥e∥22            for all ∥e∥2 ≤ r0 .                     (B.4)

In particular, lim∥e∥2 →0 ∥R(e)∥
                           ∥e∥2
                                 2
                                   = 0, so the dynamics is locally governed by the linear map J,
up to a perturbation of second order.
   We start with a local contraction result in the vicinity of the fixed-point, proved in
Appendix B.4.1.

Lemma B.1 (Local contraction near the fixed point). Let M : Rd → Rd be C 2 with a fixed
point M (x⋆ ) = x⋆ , and let J(x) denote its Jacobian. Assume that J(x⋆ ) is symmetric with
spectral radius ρ(J(x⋆ )) < 1. Then:

   1. (Local contraction.) For every ρ(J(x⋆ )) < q < 1, there exists a neighborhood U of x⋆
      such that

                             ∥M (x) − M (x⋆ )∥2 ≤ q∥x − x⋆ ∥2 ,                ∀x ∈ U.            (B.5)

      Consequently, M is a strict contraction on U, and any iterates x̂(t+1) = M (x̂(t) ) ini-
      tialized in U satisfy

                           ∥x̂(t) − x⋆ ∥2 ≤ q t ∥x̂(0) − x⋆ ∥2 ,        t = 0, 1, 2, . . . .      (B.6)

   2. (Asymptotic rate bound.) The asymptotic linear convergence rate is upper bounded by
      the spectral radius of the Jacobian, namely

                                              ∥x̂(t+1) − x⋆ ∥2
                                    lim sup                    ≤ ρ(J(x⋆ )).                       (B.7)
                                      t→∞      ∥x̂(t) − x⋆ ∥2



                                                    49
B.2     Spectral structure and asymptotic rate
We henceforth assume that the Jacobian J ⪰ 0 is real, positive semidefinite (PSD), sym-
metric and admits the spectral decomposition

                                                     J = U ΛU ⊤ ,                              (B.8)

where U ∈ Rd×d is orthogonal with eigenvectors {u1 , . . . , ud }, and nonnegative eigenvalues
{λ1 , . . . , λd } satisfying

                    Λ = diag(λ1 , . . . , λd ),               1 > λ1 ≥ λ2 ≥ · · · ≥ λd ≥ 0.    (B.9)

In particular, the spectral radius is ρ(J) = λ1 . Throughout, we assume that λ1 is simple,
i.e., λ1 > λ2 , for notational simplicity; the main results extend to the non-simple case
by replacing the eigenvector u1 with the dominant eigenspace E1 ≜ ker(J − λ1 I) (and all
projection/genericity conditions with the orthogonal projection onto E1 ).
     It is convenient to express the error in this eigenbasis. Writing et = U αt , or equivalently,
αt = U ⊤ et , and substituting into (B.3) gives

                                          αt+1 = Λαt + U ⊤ R(U αt ).                          (B.10)

Thus, in the absence of the remainder R, the coordinates {αt,i }di=1 would be decoupled ac-
cording to the scalar recursions αt+1,i = λi αt,i , with no interaction between the different
indices i. The term U ⊤ R(U αt ) represents a higher-order nonlinear perturbation that gener-
ally mixes the coordinates (i.e., introduces cross-terms between different modes). However,
this mixing vanishes asymptotically: by (B.4), its magnitude is O(∥αt ∥22 ), and hence it be-
comes negligible compared to the linear term as ∥et ∥ → 0.
Linearized Lyapunov exponent. We next formalize the fact that, in the infinitesimal
regime e0 → 0, the asymptotic exponential convergence rate is governed by the spectral
radius ρ(J). This is naturally expressed in a Lyapunov-exponent type limit (see, e.g., [25,
40]), in which we first consider infinitesimal initial errors and then let t → ∞. In what
follows, we say that a nonzero vector v is generic (with respect to the dominant eigenvalue)
if ⟨v, u1 ⟩ ̸= 0, where u1 is a unit eigenvector of J associated with the eigenvalue λ1 .
    For a given initialization e0 ∈ Rd , let et (e0 ) denote the solution of (B.2) at time t.

Theorem B.2 (Linearized Lyapunov exponent). Let J be symmetric with spectral decom-
position as in (B.8), and let u1 be a unit eigenvector associated with λ1 = ρ(J). Then

                                                        1          ∥et (e0 )∥2
                             lim          lim                log               = log ρ(J),    (B.11)
                             t→∞          e0 →0          t           ∥e0 ∥2
                                   |⟨u1 ,e0 ⟩|≥θ0 ∥e0 ∥2


for any fixed θ0 ∈ (0, 1].

   Theorem B.2 is a standard result in dynamical systems [25, 40], and is a direct conse-
quence of Theorem B.3, as stated next.



                                                             50
B.3     Finite-time linearization and iteration complexity
We now quantify how close the finite-time convergence rate is to the linearized rate, as a joint
function of the time t and the size of the initialization ∥e0 ∥2 . The next theorem (proved in
Appendix B.4.2) shows that, for sufficiently small ∥e0 ∥2 , there is a substantial time window
in which the observed exponential decay rate is arbitrarily close to log ρ(J).
Theorem B.3 (Joint control of t and ∥e0 ∥). Let J be symmetric with a spectral decomposition
as in (B.8), and let u1 be a unit eigenvector associated with λ1 = ρ(J). Assume that the
initialization e0 satisfies |⟨e0 , u1 ⟩| ≥ θ0 ∥e0 ∥2 , i.e., e0 has a nontrivial component along the
slow eigenvector u1 associated with λ1 . Fix any tolerance η > 0. Then there exist r(η) > 0,
and a minimal time Tmin (η), such that whenever e0 satisfies 0 < ∥e0 ∥2 ≤ r(η), and t is an
integer obeying
                                                                          
                                                   1                θ0
                            Tmin (η) ≤ t ≤                  log              ,                (B.12)
                                              | log ρ(J)|         2K∥e0 ∥2
            c
where K ≜ 1−ρ(J) , we have

                                   1     ∥et ∥2
                                     log        − log ρ(J) ≤ η.                              (B.13)
                                   t     ∥e0 ∥2
In particular, for sufficiently small ∥e0 ∥2 , the interval in (B.12) is nonempty and has length
of order log(1/∥e0 ∥2 ), and on this interval the observed exponential decay rate of ∥et ∥2 stays
within η of the linearized rate log ρ(J).
    The lower bound t ≥ Tmin (η) in (B.12) reflects the time needed for the faster modes
(those with eigenvalues < λ1 ) to decay, so that the dynamics are effectively dominated by
the slow eigendirection u1 . The upper bound on t ensures that the error remains small
enough for the quadratic remainder R(et ) to stay negligible compared with the linear term
Jet : the linear part decays exponentially at rate ρ(J)t , whereas the accumulated nonlinear
remainder is uniformly of order ∥e0 ∥22 , so the validity of the linear approximation persists
only up to times of order log(1/∥e0 ∥2 ). Thus, the window in (B.12) is precisely the regime
in which (i) the slowest eigenmode dominates the dynamics and (ii) higher-order nonlinear
effects remain subdominant.
    Finally, we extract a generic lower bound on the number of iterations required to reach
a prescribed accuracy, showing that the tail iteration complexity is governed by the spectral
radius ρ(J). The following corollary is proved in Appendix B.4.3.
Corollary B.4 (Tail iteration complexity in the linear regime). Suppose the assumptions of
Theorem B.3 hold, and let εabs ∈ (0, ∥e0 ∥2 ] be a target accuracy. Then, for ∥e0 ∥2 sufficiently
small so that we remain in the regime of Theorem B.3, any integer t with ∥et ∥2 ≤ εabs must
satisfy
                                                             
                                         1             ∥e0 ∥2
                               t≳                log            .                         (B.14)
                                    | log ρ(J)|         εabs
Thus, the tail iteration complexity is governed by the spectral radius ρ(J).

                                                51
B.4       Proofs
B.4.1     Proof of Lemma B.1
We prove first the local contraction result, and then the asymptotic rate expression.
Local contraction. As M ∈ C 2 , J is continuous at x⋆ . Thus, for every ε > 0 there exists
a neighborhood U of x⋆ such that
                                   ∥J(x) − J(x⋆ )∥2 < ε,          ∀x ∈ U.                             (B.15)
Choose q satisfying ρ(J(x⋆ )) < q < 1. Because J(x⋆ ) is symmetric, its spectral radius equals
its operator norm, ρ(J(x⋆ )) = ∥J(x⋆ )∥2 . Set ε ≜ q − ∥J(x⋆ )∥2 > 0. Then, by continuity,
there exists a neighborhood Uε of x⋆ such that
                           ∥J(x) − J(x⋆ )∥2 ≤ q − ∥J(x⋆ )∥2 ,             ∀x ∈ Uε .                   (B.16)
Consequently, by triangle inequality, we have,
    ∥J(x)∥2 ≤ ∥J(x⋆ )∥2 + ∥J(x) − J(x⋆ )∥2 ≤ ∥J(x⋆ )∥2 + (q − ∥J(x⋆ )∥2 ) = q < 1,                    (B.17)
which ensures that the operator norm of the Jacobian remains strictly below one throughout
Uε , that is,
                                           sup ∥J(x)∥2 ≤ q < 1.                                       (B.18)
                                           x∈Uε

   To establish the local contraction property, we apply the mean-value form of the first-
order Taylor expansion for vector-valued functions. By the fundamental theorem of calculus
applied along the line segment connecting x⋆ and x, we have,
                     Z 1                           Z 1
                          d
                ⋆
   M (x) − M (x ) =             ⋆          ⋆
                            M (x + t(x − x ))dt =      J(x⋆ + t(x − x⋆ ))(x − x⋆ )dt. (B.19)
                       0 dt                         0
Thus, for any x ∈ Uε , using the submultiplicative property of the operator norm,
                                      Z 1
                                ⋆
                 ∥M (x) − M (x )∥2 ≤      ∥J(x⋆ + t(x − x⋆ ))∥2 ∥x − x⋆ ∥2 dt
                                                  0
                                            ≤ sup ∥J(z)∥2 ∥x − x⋆ ∥2 .                                (B.20)
                                               z∈Uε

By construction of the neighborhood Uε (see (B.18)), supz∈Uε ∥J(z)∥2 ≤ q < 1. Substituting
into (B.20) gives
                                   ∥M (x) − M (x⋆ )∥2 ≤ q∥x − x⋆ ∥2 ,                                 (B.21)
which shows that M is a strict contraction on Uε with contraction constant q < 1.
   Finally, the contractive bound (B.5) implies geometric convergence by direct iteration.
Indeed, if x̂(0) ∈ U, then applying (B.5) with x = x̂(t) yields
        ∥x̂(t+1) − x⋆ ∥2 = ∥M (x̂(t) ) − M (x⋆ )∥2 ≤ q ∥x̂(t) − x⋆ ∥2 ,        t = 0, 1, 2, . . . .   (B.22)
Iterating this inequality gives
                          ∥x̂(t) − x⋆ ∥2 ≤ q t ∥x̂(0) − x⋆ ∥2 ,   t = 0, 1, 2, . . . .                (B.23)
In particular, x̂(t) → x⋆ at a linear (geometric) rate with contraction factor q.

Asymptotic rate bound. This result follows from the classical perturbation analysis of
nonlinear iterations with asymptotically vanishing remainder terms (see, e.g., [40]).

B.4.3    Proof of Corollary B.4
Fix η > 0, and assume we are in the regime of Theorem B.3, so that for all sufficiently small
∥e0 ∥2 there is a nonempty time window (B.12) on which the linear approximation is valid.

C     Local linear convergence of the population EM op-
      erator
In this appendix, we show that the population EM map M is C ∞ and derive an explicit
covariance-form expression for its Jacobian J(x) (Lemma C.3), which in particular implies
that J(x⋆ ) is symmetric, positive semidefinite, and its spectrum is contained in [0, 1]. Finally,
we combine these results to conclude the proof of Theorem 3.3.
   Throughout, S ∼ Unif{0, . . . , d − 1} denotes the latent shift distributed uniformly, and

C.1     Shift-equivariance of the population EM operator
Lemma C.1 (Shift-equivariance of the population EM operator). Let the population EM
operator M : Rd → Rd be defined by (C.3). Then:
                         M (Ts x) = Ts M (x),         ∀s ∈ {0, . . . , d − 1}.              (C.5)
Proof of Lemma C.1. First recall that each shift operator Ts is unitary: ⟨Ts u, Ts v⟩ = ⟨u, v⟩
and Ts∗ = Ts−1 = T−s . Moreover, the family {Ts } forms a commutative cyclic group repre-
sentation, so
                                          Ta Tb = Ta+b ,                                    (C.6)

                                                57
with indices understood modulo d.
   Fix s ∈ {0, . . . , d − 1}. We show two identities.
   1. Invariance of the data distribution. Set Y ′ ≜ Ts Y . Using the model and group law,

                                  Y ′ = Ts (TS x⋆ + ξ) = TS+s x⋆ + Ts ξ.                        (C.7)
                                                                   D
      Since S + s mod d ∼ Unif{0, . . . , d − 1} and Ts · ξ = ξ (because ξ is isotropic Gaussian),
                                                                       D
      it follows that Y ′ has the same distribution as Y , that is Y ′ = Y .

   2. Equivariance of the E-step ingredients. For any s, using the unitarity of Ts , we have,
                                               exp (⟨Ts Y, Tℓ Ts x⟩/σ 2 )
                          γℓ (Ts x; Ts Y ) = Pd−1
                                                                          2
                                              r=0 exp (⟨Ts Y, Tr Ts x⟩/σ )

                                            exp (⟨Y, Tℓ x⟩/σ 2 )
                                        = Pd−1                      = γℓ (x; Y ),               (C.8)
                                               exp (⟨Y,  T  x⟩/σ 2)
                                           r=0            r

      where indices are taken modulo d and we used Ta−1 = T−a and Ta Tb = Ta+b . Similarly,
      we have,

                        zℓ (Ts Y ) = Tℓ−1 (Ts Y ) = T−ℓ Ts Y = Ts T−ℓ Y = Ts zℓ (Y ).           (C.9)

                                                                                                    D
    We now compute M (Ts x) by a change of variables in the expectation. Since Y ′ = Ts Y =
Y , we have
                                     " d−1                     #

Remark C.2. The uniform prior on S and isotropy of the Gaussian noise are used to
guarantee Y and Ts Y have the same distribution, enabling the change of variables above.

C.2     The Jacobian of the population EM operator
Lemma C.3 (The Jacobian of the population EM operator). Let the population EM operator
M : Rd → Rd be defined by (C.3). Then:
  1. The map M ∈ C ∞ (Rd ) is smooth on Rd .
  2. Its Jacobian is given explicitly by
                                            " d−1                              #
                                        1    X
                      J(x) ≜ ∇x M (x) = 2 E       γℓ (x; Y )(zℓ − z̄)(zℓ − z̄)⊤ ,           (C.15)
                                       σ      ℓ=0

      where zℓ , z̄ are defined in (C.4). In particular, J(x) is symmetric for all x.
  3. At the ground-truth point x⋆ , the spectrum of J(x⋆ ) lies in spec(J(x⋆ )) ⊂ [0, 1].
Proof of Lemma C.3. We prove the lemma in steps.
Smoothness of M . For each fixed realization of Y , the map x 7→ γℓ (x; Y ) is a composition
of analytic mappings: a linear form x 7→ ⟨Y, Tℓ x⟩ followed by the exponential and a log-sum-
exp normalization. Hence, γℓ (·; Y ) is C ∞ in x. For a multi-index α = (α1 , . . . , αd ) ∈ Nd we
use the notation

We next bound ∂xα γℓ (x; Y ) uniformly over a compact set K ⊂ Rd . The first derivative of the
softmax responsibilities gives
                                              1

   Higher-order derivatives follow by repeatedly differentiating (C.17). Each differentiation
contributes at most one additional factor of σ −2 and one factor of a vector of the form (zr − z̄),
whose norm is bounded by 2∥Y ∥, while all remaining coefficients are polynomials in {γr }d−1  r=0
with magnitudes bounded by a constant depending only on α. Thus, by induction on |α|,

                             ∂xα γℓ (x; Y ) ≤ Cα,K σ −2|α| (1 + ∥Y ∥)|α| .                  (C.19)
Because Y is sub-Gaussian with finite moments of all orders, the right-hand side of (C.19)
is integrable, uniformly over x ∈ K. Since zℓ (Y ) = Tℓ−1 Y does not depend on x, dominated

The spectrum of the Jacobian spec(J(x⋆ )) ⊂ [0, 1]. Define z to be a discrete random
vector taking values in the set {zℓ }d−1 ℓ=0 , where zℓ ’s are defined in (C.4), with conditional

probabilities P(z = zℓ |Y ) = γℓ (x ; Y ). Equivalently, we have

where, S ∼ Unif{0, . . . , d − 1}, and ξ ∼ N (0, σ 2 Id ).
    Now, at the ground truth, the responsibilities γℓ (x⋆ ; Y ) coincide with the posterior prob-

                                           d−1
                                           X

                                           ℓ=0

                                 ℓ=0

conditional covariance:
                                                  1
                                     J(x⋆ ) =        E [Cov(z|Y )] .                          (C.32)
                                                  σ2
Since each conditional covariance Cov(z|Y ) is symmetric and positive semidefinite, their
expectation is also symmetric and positive semidefinite. Therefore J(x⋆ ) = J(x⋆ )⊤ , and,

                                                 J(x⋆ ) ⪰ 0.                                  (C.33)

To upper bound the spectrum of J(x⋆ ), we apply the law of total variance:

                    Cov(z) = E [Cov(z|Y )] + Cov (E[z|Y ]) ⪰ E [Cov(z|Y )] ,                  (C.34)

where both terms on the right-hand side are symmetric positive semidefinite. By the defini-
tion of z in (C.29), we have,

                      Cov(z) = Cov(TS−1 ξ) = E TS−1 (σ 2 Id )TS = σ 2 Id .
                                                              
                                                                                    (C.35)

Substituting (C.35) into (C.34) yields

                                         E[Cov(z|Y )] ⪯ σ 2 Id .                              (C.36)

Combining with (C.33) gives
                                                  1
                                0 ⪯ J(x⋆ ) =         E[Cov(z|Y )] ⪯ Id ,                      (C.37)
                                                  σ2
so all eigenvalues of J(x⋆ ) lie in [0, 1]. In particular, J(x⋆ ) is symmetric, positive semidefinite,
and orthogonally diagonalizable with real nonnegative eigenvalues not exceeding 1.

C.3     Proof of Theorem 3.3
The proof builds on Lemmas C.3 and B.1. By Lemma C.3, M ∈ C ∞ (Rd ) and its Jacobian is
given by (C.15). At x⋆ , J(x⋆ ) is symmetric, positive semidefinite, and spec(J(x⋆ )) ⊂ [0, 1].
Local contraction. Fix any q ∈ (ρ(J(x⋆ )), 1). By Lemma B.1(1), there exists a neighbor-
hood U of x⋆ such that

                         ∥M (x) − M (x⋆ )∥2 ≤ q∥x − x⋆ ∥2 ,                    ∀x ∈ U.                     (C.38)

In particular, if x̂(0) ∈ U and x̂(t+1) = M (x̂(t) ), then applying (C.38) with x = x̂(t) yields

      ∥x̂(t+1) − x⋆ ∥2 = ∥M (x̂(t) ) − M (x⋆ )∥2 ≤ q ∥x̂(t) − x⋆ ∥2 ,               t = 0, 1, 2, . . . .   (C.39)

Iterating this inequality gives the geometric bound

                        ∥x̂(t) − x⋆ ∥2 ≤ q t ∥x̂(0) − x⋆ ∥2 ,          t = 0, 1, 2, . . . .                (C.40)

Asymptotic rate. Let et ≜ x̂(t) − x⋆ . By Lemma B.1 (2),

                                                     ∥et+1 ∥2
                                       lim sup                ≤ ρ (J(x⋆ )) .                               (C.41)
                                          t→∞         ∥et ∥2

Infinitesimal initialization regime.                Apply Theorem B.2 with J = J(x⋆ ) and e0 =
e = x̂ − x , and note that et (e0 ) = e = x̂(t) − x⋆ . The genericity assumption ⟨x̂(0) −
 (0)       (0)     ⋆                            (t)

x⋆ , u1 ⟩ ̸= 0 is exactly the condition ⟨u1 , e0 ⟩ ̸= 0 in Theorem B.2. Therefore,

                                                   1    ∥x̂(t) − x⋆ ∥2
                      lim       lim sup              log (0)           = log ρ (J(x⋆ )) ,                  (C.42)
                      t→∞

which is exactly (3.10). This proves item (3).


D      EM Jacobian at low-SNR: Spectral decomposition
In this section, we derive the population EM Jacobian expansion in the low-SNR regime
x⋆ = βv with a fixed noise variance σ 2 and a varying signal amplitude 0 < |β| ≪ σ. First, we
expand the softmax responsibilities γℓ (x⋆ ) and obtain a second-order Taylor expansion with
uniform remainders (Lemma D.1). Next, we insert this expansion into the covariance repre-
sentation of the Jacobian and organize the O(β 2 ) contribution (Lemma D.2 and Lemma D.3).
Passing to the Fourier basis diagonalizes the circular–shift operators, so J(β)
                                                                            b    = F ∗ J(β)F
is block–diagonal with a 2 × 2 block for each non-mean frequency pair {k, −k}. This block
structure yields the spectral decomposition and eigenvalue asymptotics stated in Proposi-
tion 3.4 and Corollary 3.5. In turn, these results directly imply the two-phase convergence
(Theorem 3.6) and the iteration–complexity bounds (Corollary 3.8).

D.2     EM Jacobian for MRA: second–order expansion
In this section we derive the second–order expansion of the population EM Jacobian using
the second-order expansion of the EM responsibilities, derived in Lemma D.1.
Notations and assumptions. Let 1 ∈ Rd be the all-ones vector and define the (rank-one)

O(β 4 /σ 4 ).

                                              67
Step 2: Odd orders in β vanish. Any contribution to J(β) of odd order in β can be
                               (β)
expressed (after expanding γℓ (Y ) and zℓ in powers of β) as a finite linear combination of
conditional expectations of the form E [P (ξ) | S], where P is a polynomial in the noise vector
ξ of odd total degree (with coefficients depending on v and the shifts, and possibly on S).
Conditioned on S, the random vector ξ ∼ N (0, σ 2 Id ) remains a centered Gaussian, hence
E [P (ξ) | S] = 0 for every such odd polynomial P by Gaussian symmetry. Averaging over S
preserves zero, so all odd-order terms in the expansion of J(β) vanish.

Step 4: Quartic remainder. Step (2) implies that all cubic terms vanish by Gaussian

the decomposition zℓ = Tℓ−1 ξ + βTℓ−1 TS v, every term contributing to R(β, v) is of the form
                                           β4

                                           σ8
where Q is a polynomial in (ξ, v) of total degree at most 4 in ξ and at most 4 in v. In
particular, every matrix entry of R(β, v) is a linear combination of expectations of products
of the form E [ξi1 ξi2 ξi3 ξi4 ]. By Isserlis’ theorem for centered Gaussian vectors, there exists a
constant C0 = C0 (d) > 0, depending on d only, such that
                                       |E [ξi1 ξi2 ξi3 ξi4 ]| ≤ C0 σ 4 ,                    (D.50)

                                                     68
for all quadruples (i1 , i2 , i3 , i4 ). Moreover, every coefficient multiplying such moment terms
is a polynomial in v of degree at most 4, and is therefore bounded by C1 ∥v∥42 for some
C1 = C1 (d) > 0.
    Combining these bounds and summing over the finitely many index patterns, we obtain
an entrywise estimate

                                 X                                   2

D.7     Proof of Proposition 3.9
Fix k ∈ {1, . . . , (d − 1)/2} and write X⋆ [k] = rk eiϕk , with rk ≜ |X⋆ [k]| > 0, and ϕk ≜ ϕX⋆ [k].
Denote the difference of the k-th Fourier component by δk = X[k] − X⋆ [k], and assume it

                                              |δk | ≤ c rk ,                                (D.128)

for some fixed 0 < c < 1. Write

                               e−iϕk δk = ak + ibk ,           ak , bk ∈ R.                 (D.129)

                                          |δk |2 = a2k + b2k ,                              (D.130)

which follows from (D.129).
   Define the magnitude and phase errors

                        mk (δk ) ≜ X⋆ [k] + δk − rk = X[k] − X⋆ [k] ,                       (D.131)
                                          ⋆
                        pk (δk ) ≜ arg (X [k] + δk ) − ϕk = ϕX [k] − ϕX⋆ [k],               (D.132)

where arg(·) ∈ (−π, π] denotes the principal argument. Before proving the proposition, we
state and prove an auxiliary lemma.

                                          2 rk
with remainders as in (D.167)–(D.168).

Absorbing these into a single constant C > 0 (depending only on x⋆ and d) yields the desired

    Finally, since F ∗ is unitary, for every k we have |δk | = |(F ∗ e)[k]| ≤ ∥e∥2 . Let rmin ≜
min1≤k≤(d−1)/2 |X⋆ [k]| > 0. Choose ρ ≤ c rmin , so that ∥e∥2 ≤ ρ implies |δk | ≤ c |X⋆ [k]|

for a constant C depending only on x⋆ and d. This proves (3.30)–(3.31) and completes the
proof.

E.1    Oddness of the population EM map

                                         Yβ = TS (βv) + ξ,                                (E.1)

map (2.15), parameterized for each β ∈ R,
                              d−1

hence its Taylor expansion at 0 contains only odd powers of β.

Our arguments rely on standard non-asymptotic tools from empirical process theory and
concentration of measure, see, e.g., [60].

Definition F.3 (ε-net and covering number [60]). Fix ε > 0. A set N ⊆ T is an ε-net of
(T, d) if for every t ∈ T there exists s ∈ N such that d(t, s) ≤ ε. The covering number of

G     Finite-sample analysis
This appendix proves the finite–sample main results for EM in MRA at low SNR and as-
sembles them into the main lower–bound conclusions.

G.1     Proof of Theorem 6.2
We prove Theorem 6.2 in two steps.
Step 1: One–step deviation recursion. Let et ≜ ∥x̂(t) − x⋆ ∥2 . Using the triangle
inequality and then Assumption 6.1 (A1)–(A2),

      et+1 = Mn (x̂(t) ; Y) − x⋆ 2                                                                 (G.1)
           ≤ Mn (x̂(t) ; Y) − M (x̂(t) ) 2 + M (x̂(t) ) − M (x⋆ ) 2 + M (x⋆ ) − x⋆ 2               (G.2)
           ≤ sup Mn (x; Y) − M (x) + M (x̂(t) ) − M (x⋆ ) + M (x⋆ ) − x⋆                           (G.3)
             x∈B                    |          {z        } |    {z       }
             |        {z          }      (t)    ⋆               =0
                      ≤εM (n,δ) on E             ≤κ x̂     −x     by (A1)

           ≤ κet + εM (n, δ).                                                                      (G.4)

Step 2: Unrolling the recursion. By induction,
                      t−1
              t
                      X                              1 − κt
        et ≤ κ e0 +         κs εM (n, δ) = κt e0 +          εM (n, δ),      t = 0, 1, . . . , T,   (G.5)
                      s=0

                                                          εM (n, δ)

x̂(t) ∈ B for all t ≤ T on E, so that Assumption 6.1 (A1)–(A2) are applicable at each step.
This completes the proof.

                                               100
G.3     Necessary conditions on the EM sample complexity
In this subsection, we prove a general necessary condition for the sample complexity of
empirical EM fixed points. Under mild few assumptions listed below, we prove that no
empirical fixed point inside the basin can approach x⋆ beyond the intrinsic statistical noise
floor. In particular, even if EM converges algorithmically, it cannot beat the finite-sample
floor unless n is large enough.
    To capture a lower bound on the sample complexity, it is necessary to track EM along
its slowest contractive direction. Let λmax < 1 be the largest non-mean eigenvalue of the
population Jacobian J(x⋆ ) with unit eigenvector umax ⊥ 1, and define the rank-one projector

                                       P ≜ umax u⊤
                                                 max .                                 (G.15)

The subspace T = range(P ) is J(x⋆ )-invariant and the linearized dynamics reduce to

                                     P J(x⋆ )P = λmax P.                               (G.16)
                                                                              
Therefore, a lower bound on the projected fluctuation P Mn (x⋆ ; Y) − M (x⋆ ) yields, via an
amplification factor (1 − λmax )−1 , a corresponding lower bound on the attainable estimation
error.

Assumption G.1 (Conditions for the sample-complexity bound). Let B ⊂ Rd be a compact
basin containing x⋆ . Let M : Rd → Rd and Mn (·; Y) denote the population and empirical
EM operators, respectively. Let J(x⋆ ) denote the population Jacobian at x⋆ , and assume
J(x⋆ ) = J(x⋆ )⊤ ⪰ 0. Let λmax be the largest non-mean eigenvalue of J(x⋆ ) with corresponding
unit eigenvector umax ⊥ 1, and assume λmax < 1. Set P ≜ umax u⊤  max . Fix p0 > 0, and assume
that on an event Ω with P(Ω) ≥ p0 the following hold:

  1. (B0) Slow-mode gap scaling. There exist constants cλ , Cλ > 0 and an integer k ≥ 1
     such that

                                 cλ SNRk ≤ 1 − λmax ≤ Cλ SNRk .                        (G.17)

  2. (B1) Pointwise fluctuation at the truth. There exist constants c0 > 0 and α ≥ 0 such
     that
                                                 
                         P Mn (x⋆ ; Y) − M (x⋆ )        SNRα
                                                   ≥ c0  √        on Ω.            (G.18)
                                  ∥x⋆ ∥                    n

  3. (B2) Regularity near x⋆ . There exist constants KR , KE > 0 and a radius R0 > 0 such
     that for all ∆ with ∥∆∥ ≤ R0 ∥x⋆ ∥ and x⋆ + ∆ ∈ B,

                                 ∥M (x⋆ + ∆) − M (x⋆ ) − J(x⋆ )∆∥ ≤ KR ∥∆∥2 ,          (G.19)
                                                                      KE
            [Mn (x⋆ + ∆; Y) − M (x⋆ + ∆)] − [Mn (x⋆ ; Y) − M (x⋆ )] ≤ √ ∥∆∥.           (G.20)
                                                                       n

G.4      Empirical fluctuations lower bound for the MRA
Next we verify that Assumption G.1(B1) holds in the MRA setting. As in Section 3.2, we
work in the low-SNR regime x⋆ = βv and ∥v∥2 = 1, with β/σ ≪ 1.
Real-space representation of the {k, −k} Fourier block. Throughout this subsection,
we work with the real-space representation of the Jacobian eigenvectors on each non-mean
Fourier block (equivalently to the complex {k, −k} block vectors in (3.19)). Fix a non-
mean frequency k ∈ {1, . . . , (d − 1)/2} and write the DFT coefficient of v at frequency
k as V[k] = |V[k]|eiϕk . The conjugate-symmetric pair {k, −k} corresponds to a real two-
dimensional invariant subspace of Rd . Taking the inverse Fourier transform of the {k, −k}-
block basis in (3.19) yields a cosine–sine basis for this subspace. Concretely, define the real
vectors uk , wk ∈ Rd by
                  r                                      r
                     2      2πkm                          2      2πkm     
         uk [m] ≜      cos         + ϕk ,      wk [m] ≜       sin        + ϕk ,         (G.42)
                     d         d                            d         d
for m = 0, 1, . . . , d − 1, so that {uk , wk } is an orthonormal basis of this real block, and agrees
with the complex two-coordinate representation in (3.19).
    Let {Tℓ }d−1
              ℓ=0 be the circular shifts, (Tℓ x)[t] = x[t − ℓ] (indices modulo d), and set θℓ ≜
2πkℓ

     .  Then   shifting uk and wk in (G.42) simply subtracts θℓ from the phase. Using the
trigonometric identities cos(a − b) = cos a cos b + sin a sin b and sin(a − b) = sin a cos b −
cos a sin b with a = 2πkm  d
                               + ϕk and b = θℓ , we obtain for every ℓ:

                                 Tℓ uk = cos(θℓ ) uk + sin(θℓ ) wk ,                                 (G.43)
                                 Tℓ wk = − sin(θℓ ) uk + cos(θℓ ) wk .                               (G.44)

Thus, on the 2D subspace span{uk , wk }, the shift operator Tℓ acts as a real planar rotation
by angle θℓ .

constant; hence Var(Gk (g)) > 0, i.e. τk2 > 0.

                                                         111
I     Population EM approximation in low SNR
I.1    Global population EM approximation
Here, we show an approximation (developed in detail in Appendix I.2), which is not restricted
only to a basin of attraction around x⋆ , as in the local (Jacobian-based) analysis. Instead,
it provides a general low–SNR expansion of the population EM map (2.15), obtained by a
Taylor expansion of an expectation of a ratio. The expansion is carried out to second order
in ∥x̂(t) ∥/σ and ∥x⋆ ∥/σ. As we demonstrate empirically in Figure 8(a), this approximation
closely matches the true EM update as the number of observations n increases.
                                                                              (t)
    To represent the resulting approximation, we introduce the notation x̂ℓ ≜ Tℓ x̂(t) , de-
noting the cyclic shift of the current estimate at iteration t. In this context, we define the
softmax weights with respect to the true underlying signal x⋆ and the estimate x̂(t) as follows,

Figure 8: Low signal-to-noise ratio: Analytic approximation and computational cost of
                                                                                      (t)    (t)
the expectation-maximization (EM) algorithm. (a) Approximation error ∥x̂soft − x̂approx ∥2
                                       (t)
between the Monte Carlo estimate x̂soft (from Algorithm 1) and the analytic approximated EM
          (t)
update x̂approx from (I.2), which is valid as n → ∞. As n increases, the empirical estimates
approach the analytic prediction, with the error decreasing proportionally to t2 /n, where t is the
iteration number, indicating that finite sample size is the primary source of deviation. Simulations
were run with d = 8 × 8, SNR = 2 × 10−3 , and averaged over 32 trials. (b) Computational cost
in the low-SNR regime. As SNR decreases, convergence requires substantially more iterations.
Results are based on the analytic update from (I.2), corresponding to the n → ∞ regime, with the
same image size d = 8 × 8.


    Since the analytic approximation is derived in the asymptotic regime n → ∞, while
simulations are performed at finite n, the observed discrepancy can be attributed to two
main sources: (1) the inherent error in the analytic expansion itself, and (2) finite-sample
effects arising from a limited number of observations. The latter is expected to introduce
an error of order O(1/n) per iteration. As these errors accumulate across iterations, the
total approximation error grows approximately as O(t2 /n), a behavior similar to the error
accumulation seen in the Einstein from Noise phenomenon discussed in Section 5.
    The results in Figure 8(a) show that the approximation becomes increasingly accurate as
n grows, indicating that the dominant source of error is due to finite-sample effects rather
than limitations of the analytic expansion. Moreover, within the range of n considered, no
saturation in this trend is observed, further supporting the validity of the analytic approxi-
mation in the large-n regime.
Computational complexity in the low-SNR regime. In Figure 8(b), we use the ana-
lytic approximation from (I.2) to investigate the convergence behavior of the EM algorithm
as n → ∞ in the low-SNR regime. Starting from a random initialization, we simulate EM
updates using (I.2) across several SNR levels. The results show that the number of itera-
tions needed for convergence grows rapidly as the SNR decreases. In some cases, it requires
hundreds of thousands of iterations to converge. Consequently, computational constraints
may pose a significant barrier to applying the EM algorithm effectively in low-SNR settings.

                                                                     117
J     Preliminaries: Einstein from Noise
Before we delve into the proofs of Theorems 5.1 and 5.4, we fix several notations and defini-
tions, and prove auxiliary results that will be used in the proofs. For simplicity of notations,
in the single-iteration analysis, we omit the explicit dependence on the iteration index and
denote x = x̂(t) , and x̂ = x̂(t+1) for two successive iterations. Furthermore, throughout the
Einstein from Noise analysis, we will assume that σ 2 = 1.

J.1     Notations
We derive the EM update rule in the Einstein from Noise regime, when the SNR is zero.
Recall the definitions of γℓ (x̂(t) , yi ), and x̂(t) = x in (2.5) and (2.6), respectively. In the
absence of a true signal (i.e., SNR = 0), we have yi = ξi , and the the update equations of
the soft-assignment weights simplifies as follows,
                                                     exp ξi⊤ (Tℓ x) /σ 2
                                                                         
                         γi,ℓ ≜ γℓ (x, ξi ) = Pd−1             ⊤

                                                        exp  ξ   (T  x) /σ 2
                                                    r=0       i    r

for i = 0, . . . , n − 1, and ℓ = 0, . . . , d − 1. Thus, the corresponding estimator x̂ is then given
by,
                                                n   d−1
                                             1 XX
                                      x̂ ≜             γi,ℓ Tℓ−1 ξi .                           (J.2)
                                             n i=1 ℓ=0

Fourier space conjugate-symmetry relation. Recall the definitions of the Fourier
transforms of x and ξi from (5.2), and recall that the signal length d is assumed to be even.
Note that since x and ξi are real-valued, their Fourier coefficients satisfy the conjugate-
symmetry relation:
                              X[k] = X[d − k],        Ni [k] = Ni [d − k].                      (J.3)
In particular, |Ni [k]| = |Ni [d − k]| and ϕNi [k] = −ϕNi [d − k], which implies that only the first
d/2 + 1 components of N[k] are statistically independent.
The estimator in Fourier space. With the definitions above, the estimation process can
be expressed conveniently in the Fourier domain. Since a cyclic shift in the signal domain
corresponds to multiplication by a linear phase factor in the Fourier domain, the EM update
in (2.6) admits an equivalent frequency-domain representation. In particular, by linearity
of the Fourier transform and its shift property, the k th Fourier coefficient of the updated
estimate can be written as:
                                     n d−1                         
                                 1 XX                          2πkℓ
                         X̂[k] =           γi,ℓ · Ni [k] exp j        .                 (J.4)

                                    n i=1 ℓ=0

for k ∈ [d]. It is important to note that γi,ℓ captures the dependency on the estimates from
the previous iteration x, as well as the connections between the different spectral components.

J.2    Cyclo-stationary correlations
We will rely on the observation that correlation terms ξ ⊤ (Tℓ x) between the noise ξ and
the different shifts of the signal x, form a Gaussian random vector, which we denote by S.
Specifically, the rth entry of this vector is defined as,
                                                        Sr ≜ ⟨ξ, Tr x⟩,                             (J.10)

                                                              119
for 0 ≤ r ≤ d − 1. In the Fourier domain, this expression becomes,

                         Sr ≜ ⟨ξ, Tr x⟩
                            = ⟨F {ξ} , F {Tr x}⟩
                                d−1                                                
                                X                             2πkr
                            =         |X[k]| |N[k]| cos            + ϕN [k] − ϕX [k]
                                k=0
                                                                d
                                d−1
                                X
                            =         |X[k]| |N[k]| cos (ϕr [k]),                       (J.11)
                                k=0


where ϕr is defined in (J.7). The vector S ≜ (S0 , S1 , . . . , Sd−1 )T follows a multivariate
Gaussian distribution N (0, Σ) where the covariance matrix Σ is circulant. Consequently,
S forms a cyclo-stationary Gaussian process. Moreover, the eigenvalues of Σ are defined
by {|X| [k]2 }k∈[d] [7]. Therefore, under the assumption that X[k] ̸= 0 for every k ∈ [d], the
covariance matrix Σ is full-rank with rank d.

J.6    Positive definiteness of the responsibility covariance matrix
Lemma J.6 (Positive definiteness of the responsibility covariance matrix). Fix d ≥ 2 and
let x ∈ Rd be a nonzero signal with DFT coefficients X[k]. Assume that X[k] ̸= 0 for all
k = 0, . . . , d − 1. Recall the definition γℓ ≜ γℓ (x, ξ) from (J.1), and define the matrix
C(x) ∈ Rd×d by

               [C(x)]ℓ1 ,ℓ2 ≜ E [γℓ1 (x, ξ) γℓ2 (x, ξ)] ,            ℓ1 , ℓ2 ∈ {0, 1, . . . , d − 1}.   (J.44)

Then C(x) is symmetric positive definite; that is,

                               v ⊤ C(x) v > 0,             for all v ∈ Rd \ {0}.                        (J.45)

Moreover, C(x) is circulant with strictly positive eigenvalues, and in particular
                      d−1                    
                      X                2πkτ
                             cos                  E [γ0 γτ ] > 0,       k = 0, . . . , d − 1.           (J.46)
                      τ =0
                                         d

Proof of Lemma J.6. Define the random vector γ(x, ξ) ∈ Rd as the concatenation of the
responsibilities:

                                          γ(x, ξ) ≜ (γ0 , γ1 , . . . , γd−1 ).                          (J.47)

By construction,

                                       v ⊤ C(x) v = E (v ⊤ γ(x, ξ))2 ≥ 0,
                                                                   
                                                                                                        (J.48)

so C(x) is always symmetric positive semidefinite. To establish positive definiteness, it
suffices to show that

                                        v ⊤ C(x) v = 0          =⇒     v = 0.                           (J.49)

   Suppose v ⊤ C(x) v = 0 for some v ∈ Rd . Then

                              0 = v ⊤ C(x) v = E (v ⊤ γ(x, ξ))2
                                                               
                                                                                                        (J.50)

implies that v ⊤ γ(x, ξ) = 0 almost surely in ξ.




                                                          125
Positive definiteness of C(x). Define the score vector z(ξ) ∈ Rd by
                              zℓ (ξ) ≜ ξ ⊤ Tℓ x,          ℓ = 0, . . . , d − 1,              (J.51)
so that z(ξ) is a linear transform of ξ:
                                             z(ξ) = L ξ,                                     (J.52)
where the ℓ-th row of L is (Tℓ x)⊤ . The columns of L⊤ are the shifted copies Tℓ x, so L is
precisely the circulant matrix generated by x. By our assumption that X[k] ̸= 0 for all k,
this circulant matrix is invertible (see Section J.2). Consequently,
                           z(ξ) = Lξ ∼ N (0, Σz ),             Σz = LL⊤ ≻ 0,                 (J.53)
and z has a Gaussian density that is strictly positive everywhere on Rd . In particular, the
support of z is all of Rd .
    By definition (J.1), each γℓ (x, ξ) is a smooth function of the scores {zℓ (ξ)}d−1

K.1      Proof of Theorem 5.1
Contraction of Fourier components. We begin by establishing the convergence of the
magnitudes of the estimator. By Proposition J.7, whose assumptions are satisfied in our
setting, we have,
                                                  a.s.     (t)
                                      X̂(t+1) [k] −−→ αk X̂(t) [k],                             (K.1)
                      (t)
for a real constant αk ∈ (0, 1). This directly proves the magnitude convergence result stated
in (5.3).
Fourier phases preservation. Next, we address the convergence of the Fourier phases.
       (t)
Since αk > 0, the phase of X̂(t+1) [k] converges to the phase of X̂(t) [k]. Specifically, we obtain,
                                                    a.s.
                                       ϕX̂(t+1) [k] −−→ ϕX̂(t) [k],                             (K.2)

which establishes (5.4).
Positive correlation. Finally, by Lemma J.8, which holds under the same assumptions,
we have,

                                          ⟨x̂(t+1) , x̂(t) ⟩ > 0,                               (K.3)

which confirms (5.5) and completes the proof of Theorem 5.1.

K.2      Proof of Corollary 5.2
Multi-iteration Fourier phases. Denote by x̂(t) the tth iteration of the population EM
map in (2.15), and denote the initialization by x̂(0) . According to Theorem 5.1, and under
the assumptions of the proposition, for consecutive iterations, as n → ∞,
                                                    a.s.
                                       ϕX̂(t+1) [k] −−→ ϕX̂(t) [k].                             (K.4)

Therefore, by induction, we have,
                                                                 a.s.
                                      ϕX̂(T ) [k] − ϕX̂(0) [k] −−→ 0,                           (K.5)

for every T < ∞.
Magnitudes converge to zero as t → ∞. Fix a frequency k ∈ {1, . . . , d − 1} and define
                2                                                                (t)
rt ≜ X̂(t) [k] . By Theorem 5.1(1), for every t ≥ 0 there exists αk ∈ (0, 1) such that
               (t)                         (t)
X̂(t+1) [k] = αk X̂(t) [k], hence rt+1 = (αk )2 rt . In particular, (rt )t≥0 is strictly decreasing and
bounded below by 0, and therefore converges to a limit r∞ ≥ 0.
     We claim that r∞ = 0. Suppose for contradiction that r∞ > 0. Then rt ≥ r∞ /2 for
all sufficiently large t. Since the population EM map is continuous and the population
iterates remain bounded (indeed, by Theorem 5.1(3) the iterates form a positively correlated

                                                   132
contracting trajectory, so ∥x̂(t) ∥ is nonincreasing and thus uniformly bounded), the sequence
{x̂(t) }t≥0 has at least one limit point, say x∞ along a subsequence. By construction,
                                                     2
                                           X̂∞ [k]       = r∞ > 0.                          (K.6)

The contraction factor αk (x) defining the population update at frequency k is a continuous
function of x, and by Theorem 5.1(1) satisfies αk (x) ∈ (0, 1) whenever X[k] ̸= 0. Hence
αk (x∞ ) < 1, and by continuity there exist η > 0 and t0 such that
                              (t)
                            αk = αk (x(t) ) ≤ 1 − η,                     ∀ t ≥ t0 .         (K.7)

                                    rt ≤ (1 − η)2(t−t0 ) rt0 −−−→ 0,                        (K.8)

                                           lim X(t) [k] = 0.                                (K.9)
                                           t→∞

M.2.2    Successive population EM iterations
Next, we analyze the relation between two successive iterations of Ck (x), assuming the
successive iterations follow the population EM map. Explicitly, for x̂ = M (x), where M is
the population EM map we would analyze the scaling of |Ck (x̂) − Ck (x)|.


                                                          140
Lemma M.5 (One-step population drift of Ck in the low-magnitude regime). For x ∈ Rd
recall the definition of Ck (x) from (M.15). Let x̂ = M (x) be the population EM update, and
x = βv with β → 0, and ∥v∥ ≤ P . Assume that mink̸=0 |V[k]| ≥ c0 > 0. Then there exist
constants β0 > 0 and C < ∞ (depending only on d, k, c0 , P ) such that for all 0 < β ≤ β0

                                     |Ck (M (x)) − Ck (x)| ≤ C β 4 .                        (M.33)

Proof of Lemma M.5. Let x = βv and x̂ = M (x) and write x̂ = βv̂. By (M.16)–(M.17),

                                     β 4 ak (v) + O(β 6 )      ak (v)
                      Ck (βv) =      2                 2
                                                          = β2        + O(β 4 ).            (M.34)
                                    β bk (v) (1 + O(β ))       bk (v)

where we have defined,
                                                d−1
                                                                             !
                                                X   1
                       ak (v) ≜ Var |N[k]|               ⟨ξ, Tℓ v⟩2 sin(ϕℓ [k]) ,           (M.35)
                                                ℓ=0
                                                    2d
                        bk (v) ≜ |V[k]|2 .                                                  (M.36)

Define ck (v) ≜ ak (v)/bk (v); by the nondegeneracy assumption, mink̸=0 |V[k]| ≥ c0 > 0, we
have, ck (v) ∈ (0, ∞).
    The drift bound in Theorem 4.1 and (4.2) implies that the nonzero-frequency part of
x̂ − x has norm O(β 3 ). Equivalently, after rescaling by β,

                                    ∥(I − Πmean )(v̂ − v)∥ ≤ C0 β 2 .                       (M.37)

In particular, for fixed k ̸= 0,

                                   |V̂[k] − V[k]| ≤ ∥v̂ − v∥ ≤ C0 β 2 .                     (M.38)

    The map v 7→ bk (v) = |V[k]|2 (M.36) is smooth and Lipschitz on {∥v∥ ≤ P }. Moreover,
ak (v) (M.35) is a variance of a polynomial functional of v, hence it is also smooth and
Lipschitz on bounded sets. Therefore, on any neighborhood where bk (v) is bounded away
from 0, the ratio ck (v) = ak (v)/bk (v) is Lipschitz. Since bk (v) > 0 is fixed, there exists ρ > 0
and Lk < ∞ such that whenever ∥u − v∥ ≤ ρ,

                                    |ck (u) − ck (v)| ≤ Lk ∥u − v∥.                         (M.39)

For β small enough, (M.37) ensures ∥v̂ − v∥ ≤ ρ, so (M.39) applies.
   By (M.34), we have,

                     Ck (βv̂) − Ck (βv) = β 2 ck (v̂) − ck (v) + O(β 4 ).
                                                              
                                                                                            (M.40)

By (M.39) and (M.37), |ck (v̂) − ck (v)| ≤ Lk ∥v̂ − v∥ ≤ C β 2 . Hence

                                      |Ck (βv̂) − Ck (βv)| ≤ C β 4 .                        (M.41)

which is (M.33), completing the proof.

                                                   141
M.2.3    Successive empirical EM iterations
Next, we analyze the relation between two successive iterations of Ck (x), assuming the
successive iterations follow the empirical EM map. Explicitly, for x̂ = Mn (x), where Mn is
the empirical EM map we would analyze the scaling of |Ck (x̂) − Ck (x)|.
Lemma M.6 (One-step empirical drift of Ck in the low-magnitude regime). Recall Ck (·)
from (M.15). Fix k ̸= 0 and let x = βv with β → 0 and ∥v∥2 ≤ P . Assume mink̸=0 |V[k]| ≥
c0 > 0. Let x̂pop ≜ M (x) and x̂ ≜ Mn (x). There exist constants β0 > 0 and C < ∞
(depending only on d, k, P, c0 ) such that for all 0 < β ≤ β0 ,

                             |Ck (x̂) − Ck (x)| ≤ C β 4 + C β ∥x̂ − x̂pop ∥2 .            (M.42)

In particular, on any event on which ∥Mn (x) − M (x)∥2 ≤ c β 3 , we have

                                    |Ck (Mn (x)) − Ck (x)| ≤ C ′ β 4 .                    (M.43)

Proof of Lemma M.6. Write x̂pop = M (x) and x̂ = Mn (x). By the triangle inequality,

                 |Ck (x̂) − Ck (x)| ≤ |Ck (x̂pop ) − Ck (x)| + |Ck (x̂) − Ck (x̂pop )|.   (M.44)

The first term is the population drift, bounded by Lemma M.5:

                                      |Ck (x̂pop ) − Ck (x)| ≤ C β 4 .                    (M.45)

   For the second term, we use the low-magnitude expansion (M.34): for any y with ∥y∥2 ≤
2β we may write y = ∥y∥2 u with ∥u∥2 = 1 and obtain
                                                                             ak (u)
                         Ck (y) = ∥y∥22 ck (u) + O(∥y∥42 ),       ck (u) =          ,     (M.46)
                                                                             bk (u)
where ak (·), bk (·) are defined in (M.35)–(M.36). On the set {∥u∥2 ≤ P, bk (u) ≥ c20 }, the map
u 7→ ck (u) is Lipschitz, hence y 7→ Ck (y) is locally Lipschitz with constant O(β) on the ball
{∥y∥2 ≤ 2β}. Therefore, for β small enough (so that ∥x̂∥2 , ∥x̂pop ∥2 ≤ 2β),

                               |Ck (x̂) − Ck (x̂pop )| ≤ C β ∥x̂ − x̂pop ∥2 ,             (M.47)

for a constant C = C(d, k, P, c0 ). Combining (M.44)–(M.47) gives (M.42). If additionally
∥x̂ − x̂pop ∥2 ≤ c β 3 , then the second term is O(β 4 ) and (M.43) follows.

M.4       Proof of Proposition 6.6
Fix t ∈ {0, . . . , T − 1} and Let x = x̂(t) and write x = βv with β = ∥x∥2 and ∥v∥2 = 1. By
Proposition 6.5 and Remark M.7, the one-step phase-drift constant is
                                                     2
                                                (t)
                                                    σA,k (x̂(t) )
                                          Ck (x̂ ) = 2            .                               (M.50)
                                                    µB,k (x̂(t) )

Step 1: Low–magnitude scaling Ck (x̂(t) ) = Θ(β 2 ). Under the low–magnitude regime
of Theorem 5.3, we have β → 0. Applying Lemma M.4 with x = βv yields

                               µB,k (βv) = |X[k]| (1 − |X[k]|2 + O(β 4 )),                        (M.51)
                                2
                               σA,k (βv) = β 4 ak (v) + O(β 6 ),                                  (M.52)

where ak (v) is the variance functional in (M.17). Since |X[k]| = β|V[k]| and |V[k]| > 0 for
the considered frequency, we obtain

                                   µ2B,k (βv) = β 2 |V[k]|2 (1 + O(β 2 )).                        (M.53)

Therefore,

                                     β 4 ak (v) + O(β 6 )       ak (v)
                       Ck (βv) =    2        2          2
                                                          = β2         + O(β 4 ).                 (M.54)
                                   β |V[k]| (1 + O(β ))        |V[k]|2

On any set where |V[k]| ≥ c0 > 0 and ak (v) ∈ (0, ∞) (which holds for all non-degenerate
modes), the ratio ak (v)/|V[k]|2 is bounded above and below by positive constants, hence
Ck (x̂(t) ) = Θ(β 2 ) = Θ(∥x̂(t) ∥22 ), proving (6.17).
Step 2: Slow variation across empirical iterates. Apply Lemma M.6 to the empirical

                                                              144
N      Mitigation of initialization bias using mini-batch op-
       timization
In this section, we analyze mini-batch gradient methods as an alternative optimization ap-
proach aimed at mitigating Einstein from Noise phenomenon. Interestingly, while these
methods do not exhibit the Ghost of Newton phenomenon (that is, they do not initially con-
verge near Newton’s ground truth image and then diverge), the final reconstructions they
produce is similar to the diverged outputs typically seen after EM has drifted away from
Newton’s ground truth image.
    We emphasize that, although mini-batching is a standard ingredient in stochastic opti-
mization, in low-SNR MRA it mitigates a model-specific failure mechanism. In the Einstein
from Noise regime, full-batch EM updates preserve the Fourier phases of the current iterate.
This “phase-locking” behavior promotes template imprinting: spurious structure aligned
with the initialization can be repeatedly reinforced across iterations even when the observa-
tions contain no signal. Mini-batching disrupts this reinforcement by making each update
depend on a different random subset of observations, thereby perturbing the empirical align-
ment statistics from iteration to iteration and weakening the systematic propagation of the
template’s Fourier phases. As a result, the reconstruction exhibits substantially less bias
toward the initial template in the Einstein-from-Noise regime, consistent with Figure 9(b).

N.1     The mini-batching procedure description
In the mini-batching approach, only a small portion of the dataset, called a batch, is used
at each iteration, rather than the entire dataset. As analyzed in Section 5, the MSE of the
Einstein from Noise effect, after t iterations, scales as t2 /n, where n is the total number of
observations. This scaling suggests a dual benefit to using mini-batches: first, each iteration
relies on fewer observations, which reduces the cumulative bias from previous estimates;
second, more iterations are performed, mitigating the bias even further over time. As a
result, the final structure is significantly less biased toward the initial template. Indeed,
Figure 9 demonstrates that, in the Einstein from Noise regime, mini-batching results in a
substantially lower bias toward the initial template compared with the EM algorithm.
Mini-batching gradient-descent algorithms. To empirically assess the effectiveness of
the mini-batching strategy in mitigating the Einstein from Noise phenomenon, we employ
a momentum-based mini-batch optimization procedure, as outlined in Algorithm 3. This
class of optimizers aims to maximize the same log-likelihood function as EM but uses gra-
dients computed from mini-batches instead of the full dataset. In contrast to full-batch EM
and hard-assignment methods, which process the entire dataset in each iteration, mini-batch
methods update the parameters using only a subset of the data at each step. In our experi-
ments, we focus on stochastic gradient descent (SGD) variants that incorporate momentum
and adaptive learning rates, such as ADAM [28], Ranger [65], and Yogi [68]. We empha-
size that mitigating the Einstein from Noise effect through the mini-batching optimization
technique is a fundamental property, not merely an artifact of implementation choices, as
elaborated in the previous paragraph. Further implementation details are provided in Ap-
pendix N.2.

Comparison between EM, hard-assignment, and mini-batch SGD. Figure 9 com-
pares the reconstruction performance of EM, hard-assignment, and a momentum-based mini-
batch SGD algorithm across a range of SNR values, illustrating the behavior of each method
in the different regimes. For the SGD-based algorithm (the Ranger optimization method),
we use mini-batches of size 256 and run for 800 iterations with a learning rate schedule based
on exponential decay.
    In the low-SNR regime, the mini-batch SGD method performs comparably to EM and sig-
nificantly outperforms the hard-assignment variant. This is consistent with the fact that both
EM and mini-batch SGD aim to optimize the same log-likelihood objective (2.7), whereas
the hard-assignment objective function (2.9) diverges from this objective under high noise
due to its reliance on hard label assignments. In the high-SNR regime, where noise plays a
less dominant role, the performance of EM and hard-assignment algorithms is equal, while
the SGD-based method may exhibit slightly reduced reconstruction accuracy.

Figure 9: Comparison of EM, hard-assignment, and mini-batch SGD-based recon-
struction algorithms across varying SNR regimes. (a) In the Einstein from Noise regime,
the Ranger-based method exhibits the least bias toward the initial template (Einstein). In the
Ghost-of-Newton regime, both EM and Ranger recover the ground truth structure (Newton), while
the hard-assignment method erroneously converges to the initialization (Einstein). Visually, EM
achieves higher reconstruction accuracy than Ranger in this setting. In the high-SNR regime, cor-
responding to the non-coherent integration limit, the EM and hard-assignment methods perform
similarly, while Ranger shows slightly reduced reconstruction accuracy. The simulation parameters
are: image size of d = 64 × 64, number of observations n = 2 × 104 , and T = 200 iterations.
Each data point is averaged over 30 Monte Carlo trials. (b) Effect of batch size on bias toward
the Einstein template in the Einstein from Noise regime. Three setups are compared: EM with
n = 2 × 104 observations, and ADAM with batch sizes of 1024 and 128. A clear trend emerges:
larger batch sizes increase bias toward the initial template.


    Figure 9(b) demonstrates that decreasing the batch size at each iteration significantly
reduces bias, effectively mitigating the Einstein from Noise effect. This observation is con-
sistent with the trends seen in Figure 7 and the analysis in Section 5, which indicate that the

                                                                            147
Einstein from Noise effect becomes more pronounced as the number of observations increases.
    In Appendix N.2, we compare three mini-batch SGD optimizers: ADAM, Yogi, and
Ranger. While all three achieve similar levels of reconstruction accuracy, Ranger shows the
lowest bias toward the initial template (Einstein), suggesting greater robustness to initial-
ization. Moreover, none of these optimizers exhibit the Ghost of Newton effect—namely,
the non-monotonic MSE behavior observed with EM, where error initially decreases before
increasing and drifting away from the true underlying image (Newton).
Computational cost. A key advantage of mini-batching is its ability to reduce computa-
tional cost significantly. For example, using a batch size of 256 instead of the entire dataset
size of n = 2 × 104 observations enables reducing the number of observations processed per
iteration by two orders of magnitude. In our implementation, the mini-batch SGD method
was run for T = 800 iterations, compared to T = 200 iterations for the full-batch EM algo-
rithm. Despite the increased number of iterations, the overall runtime was reduced by more
than an order of magnitude while maintaining comparable reconstruction performance and
substantially reducing the Einstein from Noise bias (see additional details on computational
costs and runtime in Appendix N.2).
A hybrid approach. The complementary strengths of full-batch and mini-batch meth-
ods motivate hybrid strategies that seek to combine their respective advantages. While
both full-batch EM and mini-batch SGD aim to maximize the likelihood function in (2.7),
they differ in their trade-offs. Full-batch EM guarantees monotonic improvement of the
log-likelihood but is computationally intensive and sensitive to initialization. In contrast,
mini-batch methods offer greater efficiency and robustness to initialization, albeit without
guarantees of monotonic convergence.
    A practical hybrid approach begins with a mini-batch SGD, which reduces bias from the
initialization and moves closer to the maximum likelihood solution. The resulting estimate
then serves as the initialization for a small number of EM iterations, refining the reconstruc-
tion without requiring the full computational cost of EM with multiple iterations. Similarly,
the hybrid approach can start with small batches and gradually increase their size as the
iterations progress. This hybrid strategy is implemented in state-of-the-art cryo-EM soft-
ware pipelines. For example, the RELION and cryoSPARC software [27, 43] use a two-stage
process: first, an SGD-based algorithm with small, adaptively sized batches generates a 3D
ab initio model. This initial model is then refined using EM to obtain a high-resolution
reconstruction. The approach combines the scalability and reduced bias of mini-batching—
effectively mitigating the Einstein from Noise phenomenon—with the statistical rigor and
convergence guarantees of EM.

N.2     Empirical simulation details
Here, we detail the simulation parameters used for the reconstruction experiments employing
the SGD-based optimizers ADAM [28], Ranger [65], and Yogi [68]. For the results shown
in Figure 9(a), the following settings were used: a batch size of 256, and an exponentially
decaying learning rate of the form αt = α0 exp(−γt), with an initial value α0 = 0.95 and
decay rate γ = 0.99. The total number of iterations was set to T = 800. The momentum
parameters were β1 = 0.9 and β2 = 0.999. For the Ranger optimizer, we additionally used a

look-ahead update every k = 5 iteration with a look-ahead step size of 0.5.
   Figure 10 compares the performance of ADAM, Yogi, and Ranger in terms of their
sensitivity to the initialization of the Einstein template. All optimizers were run using the
same batch size and hyperparameters as described above. Empirically, Ranger demonstrates
the lowest bias toward the initial template, indicating greater robustness to initialization.
The reconstruction accuracy (in terms of MSE) of all optimizers is similar.
Computational running time comparison. The EM and hard-assignment algorithms
were implemented in MATLAB. For images of size d = 64×64, with n = 2×104 observations
and T = 200 iterations, a full run required approximately 15 minutes. In contrast, the
mini-batch SGD optimizers, using batch sizes of 256 observations and T = 800 iterations,
completed in about 0.5 minutes under the same conditions. All experiments were performed
on a 12th-generation Intel Core i7-12700H CPU with 32 GB of RAM.
Hard-assignment in the intermediate SNR regime. As discussed in the main text,
empirical simulations suggest that the Ghost of Newton phenomenon arises exclusively in
the EM algorithm. In contrast, the hard-assignment algorithm exhibits a sharp transition:
it moves directly from reconstructing the initial Einstein template in the low-SNR regime
(Einstein from Noise) to accurately recovering the Newton signal, without undergoing the
gradual drift characteristic of the Ghost of Newton effect.
    Figure 11 illustrates this comparison between the EM and hard-assignment algorithms
in the intermediate SNR regime. While the EM algorithm exhibits the Ghost of Newton be-
havior, the hard-assignment algorithm does not. Instead, it reconstructs the initial Einstein
template, rather than the ground truth image of Newton. This behavior persists across a
range of SNR values, as demonstrated in the simulations shown in Figure 11.

N.3                               Relation between the Ghost of Newton and Einstein from
                                  Noise regimes
To clarify how the Einstein from Noise and Ghost of Newton effects are connected, it is
useful to decompose a single empirical EM update. Recall that yi = Tℓi x⋆ + ξi under (1.1).
Plugging this into the EM update (2.6) yields the split
                                                                       (t+1)                                (t+1)
                                                          x̂(t+1) = x̂signal + x̂noise ,                                                          (N.5)
where
                                                               n X
                                                                 d−1
                                                  (t+1)    1   X
                                                x̂signal ≜             γℓ (x̂(t) , yi ) Tℓ−1 (Tℓi x⋆ ) ,                                          (N.6)
                                                          n i=1 ℓ=0
                                                               n X
                                                                 d−1
                                                  (t+1)   1    X
                                                x̂noise ≜              γℓ (x̂(t) , yi ) Tℓ−1 ξi .                                                 (N.7)
                                                          n i=1 ℓ=0

Here γℓ (x̂(t) , yi ) are the responsibilities from (2.5). This decomposition makes explicit that
EM simultaneously averages an aligned signal term and an aligned noise term, with the same
data-dependent weights.
   Figure 4(b-c) visualizes these two contributions across iterations. In the first iterations,
the estimate x̂(t) typically exhibits a hybrid appearance: it already contains recognizable

                                                                       150
Newton-like features from the aligned signal term (N.6), yet it also retains visible Einstein-
like structure inherited from the initialization. This mixture is exactly what one would
expect from the Einstein from Noise mechanism, namely, the tendency of the responsibilities
to align the noise with the current iterate, combined with genuine contraction of the signal
toward x⋆ at nonzero SNR. As t grows, the signal term continues to sharpen Newton, and
the hybrid gradually becomes more Newton-dominated.
     The crucial transition underlying the Ghost of Newton is that the aligned noise term (N.7)
evolves with the iterate. Once x̂(t) becomes sufficiently Newton-like, the same alignment
weights begin to “organize” the noise around this Newton-like structure. Consequently,
  (t+1)
x̂noise no longer resembles the original Einstein template; instead, it forms a structured,
sample-dependent perturbation that visually echoes Newton. This structured noise then
adds coherently to the update and can pull the iterate away from the ground truth, producing
the degradation seen in Figure 4(a).
     Viewed through this lens, Einstein from Noise and Ghost of Newton are two manifesta-
tions of the same underlying mechanism, alignment of noise to the current estimate, oper-
ating in different SNR regimes. In the Einstein from Noise regime (SNR ≈ 0), there is no
signal term to counteract this bias, so aligned noise alone reconstructs the template. In the
intermediate-SNR regime, the signal term initially dominates and drives the estimate toward
Newton, but the aligned noise gradually reorients to that evolving structure and eventually
induces a drift away from x⋆ .
